- **Edit note** and **Delete note** are in the same menu. Everyone editing sees notes at once; people who can only view see them but can't change them.
- Notes stay with their cells when rows or columns are inserted, deleted or sorted, Search finds their words, and they're Excel's own notes in downloads and uploads (both ways, including notes on empty cells).

## Cell edit history (Sheets)

Right-click a cell → **Show edit history** lists how it has changed, newest first: each value it had, who wrote it and when, and what it was before (*Kim Park · 10 Nov, 08:59 · was 100*). **Use this value** on an earlier one puts it back (and **Undo** takes that back too). It's read from the spreadsheet's version history, which keeps one version per editing session (when someone else edits, or after a pause), so several changes by one person in one sitting show as the last of them. For people who can edit; formulas show as written.

## Conditional formatting (Sheets)

- **Rules** in the toolbar colours cells by their value: greater or less than, between, equal or not equal to, text that contains, doesn't contain or starts with something, empty or not, or an error. Pick a colour (and bold) and the cells it applies to; the selection is filled in for you.
- **Looking at the whole range:** **Duplicate values** colours every value that comes more than once in the cells the rule covers (any case counts as the same: *Acme* and *acme*), **Unique values** the ones that come once; **Top** and **Bottom** colour the highest or lowest few numbers (ties at the edge included); **Above average** and **Below average** compare each number with the range's average. Blank cells are never coloured by these.
- **Data bars:** **Data bars (a bar by value)** puts a bar behind each number, as long as the number from 0 to the largest in the range (from the smallest when there are negatives), in blue, green, red, orange or purple, so a column of totals reads like a chart. Text and blank cells get no bar.
- **Dates:** **Date is today**, **Date is in the past** (overdue), **Date is in the next 7 days**, **Date is in the past 7 days**, **Date is in this month**, and **Date is before** / **Date is after** a day you pick (or tick **Today** for one that moves with the date). They go by today on your computer, so a tracker's colours change on their own from one day to the next; text that isn't a date is never coloured by them.
- **Colour scales:** **Colour scale (shade by value)** shades every number in the range from the smallest to the largest: red, yellow and green (or the other way round), white to green, red or blue, or blue, white and red, with the middle colour at the midpoint. Cells with text or nothing in them aren't shaded, the shades move as numbers change, and dark shades get white text so the number stays readable.
- **Custom formula is:** any formula that's TRUE for the cells to colour, written for the first cell of the range and moved along to the others like a copied formula. `$` keeps a column or row fixed, so `=$C2="late"` over `A2:F50` colours whole rows whose C cell says *late*, `=B2>AVERAGE($B$2:$B$50)*1.5` picks out large values, and `=ISEVEN(ROW())` stripes the rows. A formula that gives an error colours nothing.
- A sheet can have several rules. They're tried from the top, and the first that fits a cell colours it; the arrows change the order.
- The colours follow the values as they change, for everyone editing. Rules move with their cells when rows or columns are inserted or deleted, and carry to and from Excel (colour scales and data bars as Excel's own, today, the past 7 days and this month as Excel's "a date occurring" rules, other date rules as formula rules, duplicates and unique values as COUNTIF formula rules, top and bottom, above and below average as Excel's own, and any other formula rule from Excel as a custom formula).

## Pivot tables (Sheets)

**⊞ Pivot** in the toolbar (or right-click → **Pivot table…**) summarises the table around the cursor on a new sheet, **Pivot table 1**:

- **Rows:** one for each value of a column (e.g. *Region*); **Columns** (optional): one for each value of another (e.g. *Product*); **Values:** the **sum**, **count**, **average**, **minimum** or **maximum** of a column, or a count of rows. Each row and column has a total, and there's a grand total. Blank values are grouped as *(blank)*; numbers sort before text.
- **Several values** (**+ Add a value**, up to five) sit side by side: *Sum of Sales* and *Count of rows*, say (without a column split).
- **Dates grouped:** **Dates in it → By month / By quarter / By year** groups the row column's dates (*2026-01*, *2026 Q1*, *2026*); anything that isn't a date stays as it is.
- **Filter:** **Only rows where** a column, then untick the values to leave out (*Product*: everything but *Coffee*).
- **Show as % of the grand total** (each value its share, as a percentage) and **Order rows** by name or by the first value, largest or smallest first.
- It **updates by itself** when the data changes, including rows added below the table, for everyone with the spreadsheet open. **Edit pivot table** at the top of its sheet changes what it shows.
- Its cells can't be typed in (they come from the data). Renaming the data sheet keeps it working. Excel downloads have its values.

## Formulas that fill several cells (Sheets)

A formula that gives back several values **spills** them into the empty cells beside and below it: `=SEQUENCE(10)` fills the numbers 1 to 10 down the column, `=B2:B20*1.06` puts each price with tax next to it, `=B2:B20>100` a TRUE or FALSE for each. Hovering over a filled cell says which formula it comes from; only the formula's own cell is saved, so changing the data changes what's filled at once.

- **FILTER(range, include, [if_empty])** keeps the rows where include is TRUE: `=FILTER(A2:C50, C2:C50>100)`, `=FILTER(A2:C50, B2:B50="Sales")`. With nothing left it shows `#CALC!`, or if_empty when given.
- **UNIQUE(range)** leaves out repeated rows (any case counts as the same); **SORT(range, [column], [order])** sorts by a column (1 = first; -1 for largest first); **SEQUENCE(rows, [columns], [start], [step])** counts; **TRANSPOSE(range)** turns rows into columns.
- **SORTBY(range, by_range, [order], …)** sorts rows by other columns (`=SORTBY(A2:A30, C2:C30, -1)`: names by score, highest first; more by-ranges break ties). **CHOOSECOLS** and **CHOOSEROWS** keep the columns or rows you name (`-1` is the last), **WRAPROWS** / **WRAPCOLS** cut a list into rows or columns of a set length, **TOCOL** / **TOROW** (and Google's **FLATTEN**) put a whole range in one column or row (leaving out blanks or errors if asked), **EXPAND** makes a range bigger, **FREQUENCY(data, bins)** counts the values up to each bin and above the last, and **RANDARRAY** fills random numbers.
- **TEXTSPLIT(text, column_delimiter, [row_delimiter])** cuts text into a grid (`=TEXTSPLIT("a,b;c,d", ",", ";")`; numbers come out as numbers); **TEXTBEFORE** / **TEXTAFTER(text, delimiter, [instance])** give what comes before or after the first (or nth, or with a negative number the last) delimiter: `=TEXTAFTER(B2, "@")` is the domain of an email address. Match mode 1 ignores case; if_not_found replaces `#N/A`.
- **OFFSET(start, rows, columns, [height], [width])** points at cells a number of rows down and columns across from start, as big as you say: `=SUM(OFFSET(B1, COUNT(B2:B100) - D2 + 1, 0, D2))` adds up the last D2 months, and moves on as months are added. **INDIRECT(text)** uses a reference written as text: `=INDIRECT("B" & D2)`, `=SUM(INDIRECT("'Q1 data'!A1:A9"))`, a named range, or R1C1 style with `INDIRECT("R2C3", FALSE)`. Both give one cell's value or spill a block, work inside SUM, ROW, COUNT and the rest, and catch loops like any other formula; pointing above row 1, left of column A or at a sheet that doesn't exist is `#REF!`.
- Also: **ROMAN**, **BASE**, **DEC2BIN** / **DEC2OCT** / **DEC2HEX** and back (**BIN2DEC**, **OCT2DEC**, **HEX2DEC**, negatives as Excel writes them), **UNICODE** / **UNICHAR**, **PERMUT**, and **XNPV** / **XIRR** for cash flows on their own dates. In Excel downloads they keep the names Excel uses.
- **Functions of one value work cell by cell over a range:** `=LEN(A2:A20)`, `=UPPER(A2:A20)`, `=ROUND(B2:B20*1.2, 2)`, `=IF(C2:C20>100, "big", "small")`, `=YEAR(D2:D20)` and `=VLOOKUP(A2:A20, Prices!A:B, 2, FALSE)` fill a cell for each row (text, maths, date, IF/IFERROR/IFS and the lookups' first value; Google's **ARRAYFORMULA(…)** around a formula is accepted too). A cell that can't be worked out shows its own error (`=100/B2:B20` gives `#DIV/0!` only where B is 0), so `=IFERROR(100/B2:B20, "none")` catches them one by one.
- They work inside other functions too: `=SUM(FILTER(B2:B50, A2:A50="Tea"))`, `=COUNTA(UNIQUE(A2:A50))`, and other formulas can use the filled cells (`=SUM(G1:G10)`).
- If something is already in a cell the result would fill, the formula shows `#SPILL!` until it's cleared.
- Filled cells are printed and included in CSV downloads. In Excel downloads the formulas are kept (with the names Excel uses for them), and Excel works them out again.

## Statistics, finance and database functions (Sheets)

These give the values Excel gives (they are checked against Excel's own examples), and the newer ones are written with the names Excel expects in .xlsx downloads.

- **Distributions:** `NORM.DIST NORM.S.DIST NORM.INV NORM.S.INV STANDARDIZE` (and the older `NORMDIST NORMSDIST NORMINV NORMSINV`), `T.DIST T.DIST.RT T.DIST.2T T.INV T.INV.2T TDIST TINV`, `CHISQ.DIST CHISQ.DIST.RT CHIDIST`, `BINOM.DIST POISSON.DIST EXPON.DIST` (and `BINOMDIST POISSON EXPONDIST`).
- **Tests and intervals:** `T.TEST` (paired, equal or unequal variances; 1 or 2 tails), `CHISQ.TEST`, `Z.TEST`, `CONFIDENCE.NORM` and `CONFIDENCE.T`: `=T.TEST(B2:B20, C2:C20, 2, 1)` is the p-value of a paired test.
- **Describing numbers:** `SKEW KURT COVAR COVARIANCE.P COVARIANCE.S DEVSQ AVEDEV SUMSQ PERCENTRANK(.INC)`.
- **Lines and curves through points:** `TREND` and `GROWTH` give values on the straight line or exponential curve through known points (a cell for each new x: `=TREND(B2:B13, A2:A13, A14:A16)` forecasts three more months), `LINEST` and `LOGEST` give the line's slope and intercept (or the curve's factor and start) side by side.
- **Loans and depreciation:** `IPMT` and `PPMT` (the interest and the capital in one payment), `CUMIPMT` and `CUMPRINC` (between two payments: the interest paid in year two is `=CUMIPMT(rate/12, 360, amount, 13, 24, 0)`), `ISPMT`, `EFFECT` and `NOMINAL` rates, and depreciation `SLN SYD DDB DB`.
- **Dates and times written as text:** `DATEVALUE` reads 2026-11-09, 9 Nov 2026, November 9, 2026 or 9/11/2026 (day first unless the first number can only be a month) as a day number, `TIMEVALUE` reads 14:30 or 2:30 pm, `TO_DATE` shows a day number as a date, and `YEARFRAC` gives the fraction of a year between two dates by any of Excel's five day counts.
- **Database functions** add up a column of a table on the rows a small criteria range picks: headings over the conditions, one row per alternative (cells in a row must all hold). With *Tree* / *Apple* and *Height* / *>10* in F1:G2, `=DSUM(A1:E50, "Profit", F1:G2)` is the profit of the apple trees taller than 10. Also `DCOUNT DCOUNTA DAVERAGE DMAX DMIN DPRODUCT DGET`.
- **Arrays and others:** `MMULT` multiplies matrices, Google's `SORTN` keeps the first n rows after sorting (optionally with ties, or without repeated rows), `COUNTUNIQUEIFS`, `ADDRESS` (=ADDRESS(2, 3) is `$C$2`), `ISFORMULA` and `FORMULATEXT` (a cell's formula as text), `ISURL ISEMAIL ENCODEURL ARABIC DECIMAL`.
- Each comes up while you type a formula, with what it takes and what it does.

- **Units and working weeks:** `CONVERT(6, "ft", "m")` changes a measurement's units: mass, distance, time, pressure, force, energy, power, magnetism, temperature (`"C"`, `"F"`, `"K"`), volume, area, speed and information (`"bit"`, `"byte"`), with metric prefixes on metric units (`"km"`, `"mg"`, `"kWh"`, `"km2"`) and binary ones on bits and bytes (`"kibyte"`, `"Mibyte"`); unit names are case-sensitive as in Excel, and units of different kinds give #N/A. `NETWORKDAYS.INTL` and `WORKDAY.INTL` take your own weekend: `7` for Friday and Saturday, `11` for Sundays only (1–7 and 11–17 as in Excel), or seven 0s and 1s from Monday (`"0000011"`). They keep their names in Excel files both ways.
## QUERY (Sheets)

`=QUERY(data, "query", [headers])` picks, filters, groups and sorts rows with the same query language as Google Sheets, and spills the answer (headings first) into the cells beside and below. It follows the data as it changes.

- **Columns** go by their letters on the sheet: in `QUERY(C1:F50, …)` they're C, D, E and F. For data that isn't a plain range (the result of another function), use `Col1`, `Col2`…
- **select** the columns you want (`select *` or no select is all of them), with sums such as `C * 1.2` and `upper()`, `lower()`, `len()`, `trim()`, `abs()`, `round()`, `year()`, `month()` (counting from 0, as in Google Sheets) and `day()`.
- **where** keeps rows that match: `=`, `<>` (or `!=`), `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`, `contains`, `starts with`, `ends with`, `matches` (a pattern), `like` (`%` any text, `_` one character), `is null` and `is not null`, joined with `and`, `or` and `not`. Text matches case, as in Google Sheets; dates are written `date '2026-11-09'`.
- **group by** with `sum()`, `avg()`, `count()`, `min()` and `max()` totals each group (or the whole range when there's no group by); **pivot** turns a column's values into columns of their own (`select A, sum(C) group by A pivot B`).
- **order by** (`asc` or `desc`, several columns), **limit**, **offset**, and **label** to name a column's heading (`label sum(C) 'Total'`). `format` and `options` are accepted and left to the cells' own formats.
- **Headings:** `headers` says how many rows at the top are headings (their text becomes the result's headings; `0` means none). Left out, the first row counts as headings when it's all text and the row under it isn't.
- A mistake in the query shows `#VALUE!`, and selecting the cell says what's wrong (*There's no column Z in the data*). QUERY goes to Excel as written, but Excel has no QUERY of its own.

## Patterns, splitting and stacking (Sheets)

- **Regular expressions:** `=REGEXMATCH(A2, "^INV-\d+$")` is TRUE when the text matches, `=REGEXEXTRACT(A2, "<(.+)>")` gives the part in brackets (or the whole match; several bracketed parts spill across), and `=REGEXREPLACE(A2, "(\d+)-(\d+)", "$2/$1")` rewrites every match. A pattern that isn't valid shows `#VALUE!`.
- **Splitting and joining:** `=SPLIT(A2, ",;")` puts each piece in its own cell across (each character of the delimiter splits; `FALSE` as the third argument splits on the whole delimiter; numbers stay numbers). `=JOIN(", ", A2:A9)` joins with a separator.
- **Arrays:** `=VSTACK(A1:A5, C1:C5)` puts ranges one above the other and `=HSTACK(…)` side by side; `=TAKE(A1:D20, 5)` keeps the first five rows (`-5` the last five) and `=DROP(A1:D20, 1)` leaves out the heading. They spill like `FILTER`.
- **Choosing and finding:** `=CHOOSE(2, "low", "mid", "high")`, `=XMATCH(x, range, match_mode, search_mode)` (exact, next smaller or larger, wildcards, from the end), `ROW()` / `COLUMN()` of this cell or a reference, `ROWS` / `COLUMNS` of a range, `COUNTUNIQUE`.
- **Times and weeks:** `TIME(14, 30, 0)`, `HOUR`, `MINUTE` and `SECOND` (of a number, or text like `"2:45 PM"`), `WEEKNUM` and `ISOWEEKNUM`.
- **Cash flows:** `NPV(rate, flows)`, `IRR(flows)`, and a loan's `RATE` and `NPER`. Excel downloads keep the newer functions as Excel writes them.

## Links, sparklines and pictures in cells (Sheets)

- **`=HYPERLINK("https://example.com/orders", "Orders")`** shows *Orders* in the cell as a link that opens the address in a new tab (the label is optional; an email address opens a new email). Only web addresses and email work; anything else shows `#VALUE!`. Excel downloads and uploads keep the formula, and a cell an Excel file links to a web page or an email address (Insert → Link in Excel) opens as one of these, with its text; links to places inside the workbook keep just their text.
- **`=SPARKLINE(B2:M2)`** draws a small line chart of a range's numbers inside the cell (`=SPARKLINE(B2:M2, "column")` for columns, negative ones in red), handy beside a row of monthly figures. Cells without numbers are left as gaps.
- **Pictures in cells:** right-click a cell → **Insert picture in cell…**, choose a size (*Fit the cell*, *Stretch to fill the cell*, *Its own size*, where the row grows to fit it, or a height and width in pixels), then **Upload from computer** (kept in your Drive, next to the spreadsheet) or **Choose from Drive**. The cell holds `=IMAGE("drive:…")` (with the size as `, 2`, `, 3` or `, 4, height, width`), so it can be copied, filled and sorted like any formula. Everyone who can open the spreadsheet sees the picture, also when it's published, even if the picture file itself isn't shared with them (as long as someone who can edit the spreadsheet owns it). Only pictures kept in Workspace can be shown: `=IMAGE()` of a web address gives `#VALUE!`, so nobody's browser is sent to another site. Excel downloads leave the cell empty.

## Data from the web (Sheets)

- **`=IMPORTDATA("https://…/prices.csv")`** brings a CSV (or TSV) file on the internet into the sheet: it spills into the cells below and beside, numbers as numbers (*1,250.50* too), quoted fields with commas or line breaks kept whole.
- **`=IMPORTHTML("https://…", "table", 1)`** brings in a table from a web page (1 is the first table on the page, counted in page order), and **`"list"`** a bulleted or numbered list, as one column. A cell spanning columns repeats its text across them.
- **`=IMPORTFEED("https://…/feed")`** lists a news feed's items (RSS or Atom): title, link, date and summary (a summary written in HTML comes in as plain text). `"items title"` (or `url`, `created`, `summary`, `author`) gives one column; `"feed"` (or `"feed title"`…) the feed itself. The third argument TRUE adds a heading row, the fourth says how many items (20 unless said, up to 200): `=IMPORTFEED("https://…/feed", "items title", TRUE, 10)`.
- Use them like any range: `=SUM(IMPORTHTML(…))`, `=QUERY(IMPORTDATA(…), "select …")`. While a page loads the cell shows *#LOADING*; if it can't be had, *#N/A*, and selecting the cell says why (*The page answered 404*, *The page has 2 tables*).
- **How:** this server fetches the page (browsers can't read other sites for a page), keeps it for 5 minutes and fetches it again every 5 minutes while the spreadsheet is open. Pages up to 5 MB and 10,000 rows. Addresses on private networks (this server, your NAS, other machines at the office) are refused, so a formula can't be used to look inside your network.
- In Excel, which has no IMPORTDATA, IMPORTHTML or IMPORTFEED, these cells show an error.

## Other sheets in formulas (Sheets)

A formula can use cells on another sheet of the same spreadsheet: `=Data!A1*2`, or `=SUM('Q1 data'!B2:B9)` when the sheet's name has spaces or other characters (quote it, and double any ' in it). Sheet names match whatever the case.

- A change on the other sheet shows straight away. Inserting or deleting rows or columns there moves the references to it, and renaming a sheet renames it in every formula. A sheet that doesn't exist (or was deleted) shows `#REF!`, and a loop through several sheets shows `#CIRC!`.
- Named ranges on another sheet work the same way: `=Data!Total`.
- Excel downloads and uploads keep these formulas as they are.
- **From another spreadsheet:** `=IMPORTRANGE("link", "Sheet1!A1:C10")` takes cells from another spreadsheet, given by its link (copy it from the address bar) or its id. Leave out the sheet name for the first sheet. One cell shows as it is (`=IMPORTRANGE("link", "B2")`), and a range spills into the cells below and beside it like other formulas giving several values (see above); it also works inside another function: `=SUM(IMPORTRANGE("link", "A1:A20"))`, `=VLOOKUP("Tea", IMPORTRANGE("link", "'Price list'!A1:B50"), 2, FALSE)`. The other spreadsheet's formulas are worked out as they are there.
  - It loads in the background (`#LOADING` meanwhile) and is checked again every minute and whenever you come back to the tab.
  - Each person sees it with their own access: anyone who can't open the other spreadsheet sees `#REF!` there, so nothing is shown to someone it wasn't shared with. A spreadsheet that doesn't exist, a sheet name it doesn't have, or spreadsheets importing each other in a loop also show `#REF!`.

## Named ranges (Sheets)

Give cells a name and use it in formulas instead of the address: `=SUM(Sales)*Tax`. Select the cells and right-click → **Name this range…**, or use ⋮ → **Named ranges** to list, add, rename (**Change**) and remove them. Names use letters, numbers and _, start with a letter, and can't look like a cell (`Q1` is a cell; `Q1_sales` is fine). Case doesn't matter in formulas.

- The box left of the formula bar shows the selection's name when it has one. Type a name, a cell (`D7`) or a range (`A2:A10`) there and press Enter to go to it.
- A name nobody has defined shows `#NAME?`. Named ranges move with their cells when rows or columns are inserted or deleted, and a name whose cells are all deleted goes. Each sheet has its own names (use another sheet's as `Data!Total`).
- Excel downloads and uploads keep them as Excel's defined names.

## Named functions (Sheets)

Write a formula once and use it in any cell like a built-in function. **⋮ → Named functions** → give it a name (`WITHTAX`), its arguments (`amount`), its formula (`=ROUND(amount * 1.2, 2)`) and, if you like, a description; then `=WITHTAX(B2)` anywhere in the spreadsheet, on every sheet.

- While typing a formula, it's suggested with the built-in functions, and its arguments and description show inside its brackets.
- **Change** it and every cell using it is worked out again, for everyone with the spreadsheet open; **Remove** takes it away (cells using it then show `#NAME?`).
- Its formula only sees its own arguments (and the sheet's named ranges), so it works the same wherever it's used; it can use LET, LAMBDA, MAP and the rest, and other named functions. One that calls itself for ever shows `#REF!`.
- Names can't be a built-in function's or look like a cell. Up to 100 per spreadsheet, 10 arguments each.
- Excel downloads keep them as Excel's LAMBDA names (Formulas → Name Manager), and LAMBDA names in Excel files you upload come in as named functions. Published copies and version history work them out too.

## Protected sheets and ranges (Sheets)

**⋮ → Protected sheets and ranges** keeps cells from being changed by mistake. **Add a protection**, choose the cells (your selection is filled in) or the **whole sheet**, add a description if you like ("Prices: ask Finance"), and say who can edit it:

- **Only the owner, you, and people ticked here:** the ticked colleagues with edit access, plus the file's owner and whoever set it up. Everyone else sees those cells hatched, gets a note when they try to type there, and a whole protected sheet shows a 🔒 banner. Changes that would reach into the cells (clearing a selection that overlaps them, inserting rows above them, sorting over them) are refused as a whole. Formulas elsewhere still read them, and people can still comment.
- **Anyone who can edit, after a warning:** people are asked to confirm the first time they change those cells while the sheet is open.

Protections move with their cells when the owner (or someone allowed) inserts or deletes rows and columns. Only the people allowed to edit a protection can change or remove it. The server checks every change, so live edits, saves and restoring an earlier version can't get around a protection either.

## Mail merge (Sheets)

**⋮ → Mail merge…** (when this server has email) sends a personal email to each person in a sheet: headings in the first row (*First name*, *Email*, *Amount*…), one person per row below. Choose the column with the addresses (one called *Email* is picked for you), then write the subject and message using the headings as placeholders: `Dear {{First name}}, you owe {{Amount}}.` The **Insert** buttons put one in where the cursor is. A preview shows the email for each person (‹ ›), with values as the cells show them (formulas and number formats included); a placeholder that isn't a heading is pointed out before anything is sent.

**Send a test to me** sends the first person's email to you. **Send to N people** (after confirming) sends each one separately, from your address, with only their own address on it, up to 200 at a time; rows without a valid address are skipped and listed in the report, and copies go to your Sent folder. Each mail merge is noted in the activity log.

## Charts (Sheets)

- **Insert:** select cells (or just click one inside your table) and press **Chart**. The first row and column are used as names when they hold text. Choose **Column**, **Stacked column**, **Bar**, **Stacked bar**, **Line**, **Area**, **Scatter**, **Pie**, **Doughnut**, **Histogram** or **Waterfall**, give it a title, and check the preview.
- **The kinds:** stacked columns and bars put each series on top of the last (hovering shows each piece and the total); an area chart is a line with the space under it shaded; a doughnut is a pie with the total in its middle. A **scatter** chart plots points: across (X) is the first column of numbers when the rows have no names (heights, say, with weights going up), or the row names when they're numbers (years); both axes fit the points rather than starting at 0. A **histogram** counts every number in the cells into equal ranges with round edges (about the square root of how many numbers there are, 3 to 30 ranges; a number on the edge between two counts in the higher one), drawn as touching columns; **Show data** lists the ranges and their counts. A **waterfall** reads the first column of numbers as changes that add up (a starting balance, then what came in and went out): each step floats from the running total before it to the one after, green going up and red going down, with a blue **Total** at the end; hovering a step shows the change and the running total.
- **On the sheet:** drag a chart by its title to move it, and drag its bottom-right corner to resize it. Double-click it (or use its ⋮ menu) to change it; the menu also deletes it.
- **Reading it:** hover to see the exact numbers, and use **Show data** for the table behind the chart. Charts follow the cells, so they change as the numbers do. Rows hidden by a filter, a slicer or **Hide row** are left out of the chart.
- **Together:** charts are shared live like the cells; viewers see them but can't change them. A pie or doughnut shows the first series and puts its smallest slices together as "Other" past six. Up to eight series are drawn.
- **As a picture:** a chart's ⋮ menu → **Download as a picture (.png)** saves it with its title at 1200 × 700, in the light colours on white (even when you use dark mode), ready for an email, a report or a slide. Anyone who can open the spreadsheet can do this, viewers included.
- **In Excel:** **Download as Excel (.xlsx)** includes the charts as real Excel charts of the same kind (a histogram as columns of its counts, and a waterfall as stacked columns on a hidden base, with their figures in the chart; when the cells hold formulas, as a column chart of the cells instead), with their titles, placed where they are on the sheet. Their series point at the sheet's cells, so Excel redraws them when the numbers change there. Charts in an Excel file you open in Sheets come in too: column, bar (side by side, stacked or 100% stacked, flat or 3-D), line, area, scatter, pie and doughnut charts of cells on their own sheet, with their titles, over the same cells as in Excel. Other kinds (radar, bubble, stock…) and charts of another sheet's cells are left out; the numbers are always there.

## Slides

In Drive, **New → Presentation**. Presentations are shared, versioned and saved automatically like documents.

- **Slides:** **+ Slide** adds one after the current slide with a layout (title and text, title slide, section header, two columns, blank). Each slide's ⋮ menu duplicates, moves or deletes it.
- **Ordering slides:** drag slides up and down the list on the left. **⊞ Grid view** shows every slide at once: drag them into order, Ctrl- or Shift-click to choose several and drag them together (they keep their order), or **Duplicate**, **Skip / show** or **Delete** them in one go. Double-click a slide (or Enter) to open it.
- **On a slide:** add a **Text box**, a **Picture** (from your computer; it's saved in Drive next to the presentation) or a **Shape** (rectangle, rounded rectangle, ellipse, line or arrow; a line or arrow's **Direction** runs across, down, diagonally or backwards, and they're kept in PowerPoint files).
  - Drag to move, and drag the handles to resize. Pictures keep their shape; hold Shift to stretch them.
  - **Alt text** in the toolbar (with a picture selected) describes the picture for screen readers (✓ once it has one). It's kept in PowerPoint files both ways.
  - Double-click text to type. Arrow keys nudge the selected item (Shift for bigger steps), and Delete removes it.
  - The toolbar sets text size, bold, italic, colour and alignment, shape colour, the slide's background, and front/back order. The ⋮ menu picks one of four themes. **⋮ → Theme and logo…** makes the presentation's own: background, text and accent colours (start from one of the four and change them), a font for every slide (Arial, Georgia, Times New Roman, Verdana, Trebuchet MS or Courier New), and what goes **on every slide**: a logo in a corner of your choice and a line of footer text (*Acme Trading · Confidential*), with **Not on the first slide** for a title slide without them. A preview shows the current slide as you go. The logo and footer show when presenting, in presenter view, printed, published to the web and in **Download as PowerPoint** (with the colours and font); everyone who can open the presentation sees the logo, even if the picture itself isn't shared with them.
  - **Background picture** (toolbar, with no box chosen) puts a PNG, JPEG or GIF behind everything on the slide, filling it (a photo, a branded backdrop); **Remove background picture** takes it away, and Ctrl+Z undoes either. It's kept in your Drive, and everyone who can open the presentation sees it (viewers, presenting, published pages), even though the picture file itself isn't shared with them. PowerPoint downloads keep it as the slide's background, and PowerPoint files bring theirs in.
  - **Text in shapes:** double-click a rectangle, rounded rectangle or ellipse (or choose it and press Enter) to type in it: the words sit in its middle, in the slide's background colour so they read on the shape's colour, and the toolbar sets their size, bold, italic, colour and alignment, as for a text box. Esc finishes. Find and replace and Drive search look in shapes too, and PowerPoint downloads keep the words in their shape (a filled shape with words in a PowerPoint file comes in as one, too).
  - **Shapes:** **Shape ▾** has rectangles, rounded rectangles, ellipses, triangles, diamonds, block arrows, chevrons (for process steps), stars and speech bubbles, plus lines and arrows. All but lines and arrows can hold text (in a speech bubble it stays in the bubble, above the tail). PowerPoint downloads keep each shape as PowerPoint's own, and PowerPoint files bring these shapes in.
  - **Outlines and box colours:** choose a rectangle, rounded rectangle, ellipse or text box and pick an **Outline** colour, then its thickness (1, 2, 4 or 6 pt); a text box can also have a **Box colour** behind its words (a highlighted note, a callout). **No outline** / **No box colour** take them away. PowerPoint downloads keep both, and outlines and coloured text boxes in PowerPoint files come in (a filled rectangle whose words start at the top comes in as a coloured text box; one with its words in the middle as a shape).
- **Videos:** **Video** in the toolbar puts a video on the slide, **From Drive…** or **Upload a video…** (MP4, WebM, Ogg or QuickTime; saved next to the presentation). While editing it shows its first frame with ▶ (move and resize it like any box); when presenting, or for anyone viewing, it plays with its controls, seeking included. Everyone who can open the presentation can play it, even without access to the video file itself. PowerPoint downloads carry it as a real video (up to 100 MB; a bigger one appears as its name).
- **Sound:** **Audio** in the toolbar puts a sound on the slide (narration, music, a jingle), **From Drive…** or **Upload a sound…** (MP3, M4A, WAV, Ogg or WebM; saved next to the presentation). While editing it's a small 🔊 box with its name (move and resize it like any box). When presenting, or for anyone viewing, it's a player with play, seeking and volume. Tick **Play by itself** (with the box chosen) and it starts on its own when its slide comes up while presenting, and stops when the slide changes. Everyone who can open the presentation can play it, even without access to the sound file itself. PowerPoint downloads carry it as real sound (up to 50 MB; a bigger one appears as its name), OpenDocument ones as its name.
- **Import slides:** **⋮ → Import slides…** picks another presentation you can open (yours or shared with you), shows its slides, and puts the ones you tick after the current slide, with their text, shapes, tables, charts and speaker notes, in this presentation's theme. Their pictures are copied into your Drive (next to this presentation when you can add files there), so everyone who can open it sees them. A presentation whose owner turned off downloading and copying for viewers can't be imported from.
- **Charts from Sheets:** **Chart** in the toolbar picks a spreadsheet and one of its charts, and puts it on the slide (move and resize it like any box). The slide keeps a copy of the chart, so everyone who can open the presentation sees it, presenting included, even if the spreadsheet isn't shared with them. Select it to see whether the spreadsheet has changed since: **↻ Update chart** brings in its latest figures, title and type (for people who can open the spreadsheet; it's checked when you select the chart, come back to the tab, and every minute). **Open spreadsheet** goes to it. In PowerPoint downloads it's a real chart with those figures. Rows the spreadsheet hides (with a filter, a slicer or **Hide row**) are left out, as on the sheet.
- **Gradient backgrounds:** next to the slide's background colour, **Gradient to…** fades it into another colour (blue, purple, green, red, black, light ones…), and the direction beside it runs it **top to bottom**, **left to right**, corner to corner or upwards; **No gradient** takes it off. Everyone sees it as it changes, it shows when presenting, and PowerPoint downloads keep it as PowerPoint's own gradient (a gradient in a .pptx comes in the same way, with its first and last colours).
- **Transitions and skipping:** a slide's ⋮ menu sets how it appears when presenting (**Fade**, **Push**, **Zoom** or none; ✦ marks it in the list) and **Skip when presenting** for backup slides (faded in the list, ⊘). Presenting passes over skipped slides, in the presenter window too. Skipped slides stay hidden in PowerPoint downloads; transitions from PowerPoint files are read, but downloads don't carry them.
- **Smart guides:** while you drag a box, it lines up with the slide's edges and centre and with the other boxes' edges and centres: it snaps into place when close, and pink lines show what it lines up with. Hold **Alt** while dragging to place it freely.
- **Rotating:** select a box and drag the round handle above it to turn it (in 15° steps; hold **Shift** for any angle), or type the angle in the toolbar's ⟳ box (0 puts it upright). Text, pictures, shapes and tables can be turned; they stay turned when presenting, and PowerPoint files keep the angle both ways.
- **Several boxes at once:** **Shift**-click boxes to choose several (Shift-click one again to take it out). Drag any of them to move them all, use the arrow keys to nudge them, or **Del** to delete them. The toolbar lines them up (left, centre or right edges; top, middle or bottom) and, with three or more, **Space** spreads them evenly across or down.
- **Grouping:** with several boxes chosen, **▣ Group** (or **Ctrl+Alt+G**) makes them one group, such as a logo with its caption or a diagram's shapes and labels. A click on any of them then picks the whole group (*Group of 3*), and it's moved, nudged, lined up, copied and deleted as one; double-click one box to type in it on its own. **⧉ Ungroup** (or **Ctrl+Alt+Shift+G**) takes it apart. Copies of a group are a group of their own, and a group left with one box is no longer a group. PowerPoint and OpenDocument downloads keep groups (as PowerPoint groups and Impress groups), and groups in .pptx and .odp files come in as groups, at the size and place they show, even when PowerPoint has shrunk or stretched them.
- **Copy, paste, duplicate:** **Ctrl+C** copies the chosen box (or boxes), **Ctrl+X** cuts, and **Ctrl+V** pastes them on the slide you're on: in the same place on another slide, or a little down and right on the same one, so the copy doesn't hide its original. **Ctrl+D** duplicates at once. Text, shapes, tables and charts can be pasted into another presentation too; pictures and videos stay in their own (add them from Drive there).
- **Links:** select a box (text, picture, shape, table or chart) and choose **🔗 Link** to send it to a web page (or an email address) or to another slide of the presentation, such as a menu slide leading to each section. 🔗 marks linked boxes while editing. When presenting, a click on the box follows the link (web pages open in a new tab) instead of moving on; anywhere else moves on as usual. Deleting the slide a link points to takes the link off. Links are kept in PowerPoint files both ways.
- **Animations:** select a box (text, picture, table or shape) and pick **Animation: Appear**, **Fade in** or **Fly in** in the toolbar. While presenting, each click (or →, Space, Enter) brings in the slide's next animated box, and only then goes to the next slide; ← takes them away again, and going back to a slide shows it with everything in. Boxes come in in the order you gave them an animation, shown by an orange number on each while editing (**No animation** takes one out; giving it one again puts it last). The presenter window shows the audience's view and how many are in. PowerPoint downloads show every box, without the animations.
- **Speaker notes:** type them in the box under each slide. They're shared and saved with the presentation, carried to and from PowerPoint, and never shown to the audience.
- **Find and replace:** **Ctrl+F** (or ⋮ → **Find and replace**) searches every slide's text boxes, table cells and speaker notes. Enter (Shift+Enter) or the arrows go to the next (previous) match: its slide opens with the box chosen, and the bar shows where it is (*Slide 2 · Table, row 1*) with the words around it. *Match case*, *Whole words* and *Regular expression* work as in Docs. **Ctrl+H** (or *Replace…*) replaces the current match or all of them (`$1`, `$2`… for a pattern's parts), for everyone at once, and **Ctrl+Z** undoes it. Viewers can find but not replace.
- **Present** opens full screen. The arrow keys, space or a click move between slides, and Esc ends. **N** shows or hides your notes at the bottom (handy with a single screen).
- **Presenter view** also opens a second window for you, the speaker, to keep on your own screen: the current slide, the next one, your notes, a timer and the time. Moving through the slides in either window moves both.
- **Audience Q&A:** while presenting, **❓** in the bar at the bottom starts a Q&A and shows a panel in the corner with where to ask (*your-server/qa/ewgbc8*, a short code to read out or put on a slide). People at your company open it on a phone or laptop, type a question (with their name, or tick **Ask without my name**) and **▲ upvote** the ones they want answered (once each, not their own; click again to take it back). The panel shows them as they come in, most upvoted first, with **Mark answered** (they move to the bottom, crossed out, and show *✓ Answered* to everyone). **Presenter view** shows the same list beside the speaker notes. ✕ hides the panel (questions keep coming in, and ❓ brings it back), and **End Q&A** stops new questions; the next ❓ starts a new one. Whoever asked, or the presenter, can delete a question. Anyone who can edit the presentation can run its Q&A, and only people at your company can ask.
- **Slide numbers:** **⋮ → Slide numbers: every slide** (or **all but the first**, for a title slide) puts each slide's number in its bottom-right corner, on screen, when presenting, when printing and in PowerPoint downloads (where they're real slide numbers; PowerPoint files with them open with numbers on). **Slide numbers: off** takes them away. Everyone editing sees the change at once.
- **Moving on by itself, and looping:** a slide's ⋮ menu → **Move on by itself** (after 5, 10, 15 or 30 seconds, or a minute; ⏱ marks it in the list) shows the next slide (or the next animated box) when the time is up; moving on yourself starts the wait again. **⋮ → Loop: start again after the last slide** goes back to the first slide after the last one, for a presentation that runs by itself on a screen in reception. While presenting, **P** (or ⏸ in the bar) pauses and resumes it. Both are kept in PowerPoint files, both ways (with the slides' transitions, which now go out to PowerPoint too).
- **Laser pointer:** while presenting, press **L** (or ● in the bar at the bottom) for a red dot that follows the mouse instead of the pointer; **L** again turns it off.
- **Slides as pictures:** **⋮ → Download this slide as a picture (.png)** saves the slide you're on as a 1600 × 900 picture, exactly as it looks (fonts, pictures, shapes, the theme), for a chat, a report or social media; **Download all slides as pictures (.zip)** saves every slide, numbered so they sort (*Launch deck - 01.png*…). They're made in your browser.
- **Rehearse:** ⋮ → **Rehearse (time each slide)** presents from the first slide with a timer at the top (time on this slide, and in all). When you end (Esc or ✕), **Rehearsal** shows how long each slide took and the total, and how that compares with your last rehearsal (*1:05 quicker*). Your last five rehearsals of each presentation are kept, for you alone; going back to a slide adds to its time.
- **Print or save as PDF…** (⋮ menu) asks what to print: **Slides** (one per landscape page), **Slides with speaker notes** (each slide with its notes underneath, for the presenter), or **handouts** for the audience with **2**, **3** (with lines beside each to write on) or **6** slides per portrait page. Notes and handouts carry the presentation's title and page numbers at the top. Skipped slides are left out unless you tick **Include skipped slides**; the dialog says how many pages it will be. Choose "Save as PDF" in the print dialog for a PDF.
- People you share a presentation with see its pictures, even ones that aren't shared with them separately. Viewers can present but not edit.

## Contacts

- **Who's who:** everyone can add their job title, department and work phone in **Settings → About you**; admins can change them and set who each person reports to in **Admin → Users → ⋮ → Job and manager** (someone can't report to a person who reports to them). The **Directory** shows title · department and the phone, and **Org chart** draws who reports to whom, with a message button on each person. Typing in **Filter** narrows the chart to the people who match, shown with their managers.
- **A colleague's card:** click a name in the **Directory** or the org chart, or a message's author (name or picture) in **Chat**, for their card: job title and department, status, email and phone, their local time and whether it's their working hours (when they've set working hours), where they're working today, who they report to and who reports to them (click any of those to move to their card). **Message**, **Call** (a video call in your direct messages with them: they're rung, wherever they are in Workspace, and the meeting opens for you), **Email**, **Schedule a meeting** (a new event with them invited, at the first half hour you're both free within your working hours, to change and save) and **See their calendar** (adds them under *Other calendars*) are one click away. Only for people in your company.
- **Import:** **Import** takes a vCard file (.vcf, with one or many contacts) exported from a phone, iCloud, Outlook or Gmail, or a CSV export from Gmail or Outlook (or any sheet with a Name or Email column), or LinkedIn's *Connections.csv* (from *Settings → Data privacy → Get a copy of your data*: each connection's name, address when they share it, company, position as job title and profile link as website, with when you connected in the notes). People who are already in your contacts (the same email address) are skipped, so importing the same file twice is harmless. Birthdays, addresses and other details on a vCard are kept for your phone.
- **Export:** **Export** downloads all your contacts as vCards (for phones, Outlook and Apple) or as CSV (for Gmail and spreadsheets).
- **In Mail:** start typing a name or address in To, Cc or Bcc and pick from your contacts and colleagues (Up/Down, then Enter or Tab). Your starred contacts come first, then the people you email most often (from what you've sent in Workspace), then the rest.

- **History with someone:** a colleague's card (from the Directory, Chat and elsewhere), and the 🕒 next to a contact with an email address, show your **recent emails** with them (from them, or to or copied to them, in your Inbox, Sent and Archive; the latest ten) and your **meetings** with them: the next ones first, then the last ones (your events they're invited to, theirs you're invited to, and invitations from them by email; repeating ones by their next date). Click one to open it in Mail or Calendar.
- **Photos:** **Add photo** in a contact's window picks a picture; its middle square is kept, small (256 pixels), and shows next to the name in your contacts (and in the company's, for a shared contact). **Change photo** or **Remove photo** later. Photos travel with the contact: in vCard exports and imports, and to and from phones syncing over CardDAV (a photo set on the phone shows on the web, and the other way round; photos phones synced before this are brought out too). Mail shows a contact's photo next to messages from their address (a colleague's own profile photo comes first).
- **Sorting:** the menu beside the filter in **My contacts** sorts by **First name**, **Last name** (the last word of the name), **Company** (those without one at the end) or **Recently added**; starred contacts stay first. Your choice, kept on every device.
- **Starred:** ☆ next to a contact stars it (★): starred contacts come first in **My contacts**, and first among suggestions when you type an address in Mail. ★ again takes the star off.
- **More addresses and numbers:** **+ Add another email** and **+ Add another phone** in a contact's window add up to ten more of each, labelled **Work**, **Home**, **Mobile** (phones) or **Other**. They show in the list under the first, each address is suggested on its own when you write mail, and they go to and from phones (CardDAV) and vCard files with their labels. CSV files keep them too: Gmail's numbered columns (*E-mail 2 - Value*, *Phone 2 - Type*…) and Outlook's (*E-mail 2 Address*, *Mobile Phone*, *Business Phone*, *Home Phone*…) come in with their labels, and **Export → CSV** writes them as Gmail does. Contacts synced from phones before kept these hidden on the card; they now show. **Merge duplicates** adds the other contacts' addresses and numbers to the one you keep instead of noting them.
- **More details:** a contact can also have a job title, a website (https:// is added if you leave it off) and a postal address. They show in the list (job title · company · website) and travel with vCard and CSV files and phone sync; an address that came from a phone keeps its parts (street, city, postcode…) on the phone unless you change it here.
- **Birthdays:** a contact can have a birthday (with or without the year: tick **Year not known**). Birthdays show on the Calendar as all-day items with the age they turn when the year is known (29 February falls on the 28th in other years); click one to find the contact, or untick **Show contacts’ birthdays** to hide them. Birthdays come in and go out with vCard and CSV files and sync with phones. **🎂 Reminders** at the top of Contacts sends a notification (in the bell, and on phones with notifications on) on the morning of a contact's birthday or other date, from 9:00 in your time zone (*🎂 Today: Ada Lovelace’s birthday (36) and 2 more*), or **the day before**; one a day at most, naming everyone whose day it is. It's off until you choose, and the bell's notification settings can turn it off too.
- **Other dates and fields of your own:** in a contact's window, **+ Add a date** adds an anniversary, a work anniversary or any date you name (with or without the year), and **+ Add a field** a label and a value of your own (*Customer no.: 4711*, *Parking: B12*), up to ten of each. Fields show in the list; dates show on the Calendar with birthdays (*🗓 Anniversary: Ann Lee (11)*). They travel with vCard files and phones (dates the way iPhones and Macs keep them, so they show there) and with CSV files (Gmail's *Event* and *Custom Field* columns).
- **Groups:** the **Groups** tab in Contacts holds your own lists of people (contacts, colleagues or any address) to email together. Type a group's name in To, Cc or Bcc and pick it to put in everyone in it, or use the envelope button next to the group. **📅** next to it opens a new event named after the group with everyone in it invited, at the first hour you and the colleagues in it are free. Groups are private to you.
- **Other contacts:** people you send email to who aren't colleagues or in your contacts are remembered (how often and when you last wrote), suggested when you write (marked "Emailed before"), and listed on the **Other contacts** tab. **Add to contacts** makes one a real contact; **×** forgets one.
- **Frequent:** the **Frequent** tab lists the people you've emailed most from Workspace in the last six months (up to 20), most often first: colleagues (marked), your contacts and anyone else, with how many times and when last. **Email** starts a message to them; **Add to contacts** keeps someone who isn't in your contacts yet. It counts what you send from now on, and it's yours alone. **×** on a row forgets that person (they leave this list, and Mail stops suggesting them in *Include …?* until you write to them again); **Clear all** forgets everyone. With email on, **Home** has a **People you email most** card too: the top five, each with **✉ Email** (its heading opens this tab).
- **Company contacts:** tick **Share with everyone at {company}** on a contact (a supplier, a client, the shared phone list) and it shows on everyone's **Company** tab, with who added it, and is suggested to everyone when they write mail. Only the person who added it, or a company administrator, can change or delete it; untick the box to make it yours alone again.
- **Log:** the 🕘 next to a contact opens its history, which starts with your **Log**: write what happened (*Talked about the renewal*), pick **📞 Call**, **🤝 Meeting** or **📝 Note**, and a day if it wasn't today. Entries are listed newest first (× removes one) above the emails and meetings with them. A logged call or meeting counts as being in touch (see below); a note doesn't. The log is yours alone.
- **Keep in touch:** in a contact's window, **Keep in touch** picks how often you mean to be in touch (every week up to every year). The **Keep in touch** tab lists those it's been longer for, most overdue first, with when you last were (*never*, *3 days ago*, *5 weeks ago*): **Email** writes to them, and **✓ In touch** (after a call or a meeting) starts the clock again. Sending them email from Workspace counts by itself. It's your own, for your own contacts. Once someone you've been in touch with before is due again, the bell says so once (*Time to catch up with Rita Moreno and 2 others*); it can be turned off in the bell's settings.
- **QR code:** **▦** next to a contact (yours, or a company contact) shows it as a QR code: point a phone's camera at it and the phone offers to add the contact. **Download vCard (.vcf)** in the same window saves it as a file to email or open on a computer. Both hold the name, email addresses, phone numbers, company, job title, address and website; your notes and dates stay with you.
- **Send in Chat:** the share button next to a contact sends it into a Chat conversation (a channel or a direct message), with a message if you like. Everyone there sees a card with the name, job title and company, email addresses, phone numbers, address and website, and **Add to my contacts** puts a copy in their own contacts (if they already have someone with that email address, theirs is kept). Notes and dates stay with you. The same button is on the **Company** tab's contacts (even ones you can only view), and in the **Directory** it sends a colleague's card: their name, address, job title, department and work phone.
- **Several at once:** in **My contacts**, tick the boxes beside contacts (or the one at the top for everyone shown, filtered or not) and a bar offers **Email** (a new message to everyone selected who has an address), **Add to group…** (their addresses join one of your groups; those without one are left out, and said so), **★ Star** / **☆ Unstar**, **Export selected** (just those, as vCard or CSV, the same as **Export** gives for everyone), **Send in Chat…** (each as its own card in a conversation, with your message on the first; colleagues can add them to their contacts), **Print** (an address list of just those: name and job title, email addresses, phone numbers, company and address, ready to print or save as PDF; **Export → Print the list** does it for everyone), and **Delete** (asked first; phones lose them on their next sync). Every contact is checked first: if one can't be changed, none is.
- **Trash:** a deleted contact (one, several at once, or deleted on your phone) goes to the **Trash** tab for 30 days, with its photo and every detail, and **Undo** in the message after deleting puts it straight back. In Trash, **Restore** puts a contact back as it was (in its groups, and on your phone at its next sync), the bin icon deletes it for good, and **Empty trash** deletes them all. After 30 days they're gone by themselves. A company contact someone else deleted is in their trash, and in its owner's.
- **Undo changes:** **Undo changes** at the top of Contacts puts your contacts back as they were at an earlier time: **10 minutes ago**, **1 hour ago**, **yesterday**, **1 week ago**, or a date and time of your own in the last 30 days (after an import or a merge that went wrong, or a phone that changed them). It says first how many contacts that touches. Changed contacts go back as they were, ones added since then go to the **Trash** (so nothing is lost), and ones deleted since then come back (with their photo, while the trash still has it). Changes made anywhere count, phones included; an undo can itself be undone the same way.
- **Merge duplicates:** **Merge duplicates** finds contacts that are the same person: the same email address, the same phone number (spaces and the country code don't matter) or the same name. Pick the one to keep in each group and **Merge**. The kept contact fills in what it's missing from the others, their other email addresses and phone numbers are added to it, anything else that differs (an address, a company) goes into its notes, and the others are deleted (from your phone too, on its next sync).
## Calendar and contacts on your phone

Your Workspace calendar and contacts sync with the phone's own Calendar and Contacts apps, both ways, using the standard CalDAV and CardDAV. Settings → *Calendar and contacts on your phone* shows the details for each person.

- **iPhone and iPad:** Settings → Calendar → Accounts → Add Account → Other → **Add CalDAV Account**. Enter the server (`workspace.example.com`), your email address and password. For contacts, do the same under Contacts → **Add CardDAV Account**.
- **Android:** install the free **DAVx⁵** app → *Login with URL and user name*, with `https://workspace.example.com/`. Calendars and contacts then appear in the phone's apps.
- **Your own calendars** (Calendar → *My calendars*) arrive as separate calendars beside the main one, in their colours. Events added to one on the phone land in it; moving an event to another calendar on the phone moves it in Workspace too (guests and all); renaming or recolouring one on the phone is kept. Invitations stay in the main calendar. Calendars colleagues share with you arrive too, named *Calendar (Their name)*: read-only when shared to see, and when you may make changes, events you add, change or delete there are changed in Workspace (theirs, and they're told).
- **Thunderbird, macOS Calendar and Contacts** work the same way. The address `https://workspace.example.com/dav/` is the one to use if an app asks for the full address.
- **Password:** your account password. With two-step verification on, use a **mail-app password** instead (Settings), so a phone never gets around the second step. Wrong passwords count towards sign-in protection.
- **What syncs:**
  - Your events with your reminders, including repeating ones (daily, weekly on chosen days, monthly on a date, yearly; an end date or a number of times; skipped dates). Changes on either side show up on the other.
  - Your contacts. Addresses, birthdays and extra numbers added on a phone are kept, even though the web only shows one email and one phone number.
  - **Invitations** from colleagues appear on your phone with the organiser and guests. Accept, Maybe or Decline there is your answer in Workspace, and deleting one declines it. You can't change someone else's event from the phone.
  - **One occurrence of a repeating event** changed on its own (on the phone, or with "This event" on the web) shows on both as part of the series. Cancelling one on the phone skips that date.
  - **Time zones** come across however the app names them: standard names (`Europe/Berlin`), Outlook's Windows names (`W. Europe Standard Time`), or a zone the app describes itself (Outlook's "Customized Time Zone"), so a 9:00 meeting stays at 9:00.
  - **The second Monday, the last Friday:** monthly repeats on a weekday go both ways (as phones and Outlook write them).
- **Not yet:**
  - Repeats that Workspace can't show (such as "the fifth Monday" or "every Monday and Wednesday of the month") arrive as a one-off on the first date.
- **Synology reverse proxy:** nothing to add. The same HTTPS entry serves `/dav/` and the `/.well-known/caldav` and `/.well-known/carddav` addresses that apps look for.

## Word, Excel, PowerPoint and OpenDocument files

- **Previewing:** opening a `.docx`, `.odt`, `.xlsx`, `.ods`, `.pptx` or `.odp` file in Drive (or an email or chat attachment) shows it read-only in the preview, as Docs, Sheets or Slides would (up to 20 MB; nothing is copied). Anyone it's shared with can look, view-only files included.
- **Opening:** for a `.docx`, `.odt`, `.md`, `.xlsx`, `.ods`, `.pptx` or `.odp` file in Drive, pick **Open as document / spreadsheet / presentation** (⋮ menu, or the button in its preview). Workspace makes an editable Docs, Sheets or Slides copy next to it, and the original stays as it is.
- **Downloading:** **Download as Word / Excel / PowerPoint** is in each editor's ⋮ menu (documents also as OpenDocument (.odt) and Markdown, spreadsheets and presentations also as OpenDocument (.ods, .odp)). It includes what's just been typed, even by someone else in the last moment.
- **What carries over:**
  - **Word:** headings, paragraphs, bold, italic, underline, strike, links, bulleted and numbered lists, quotes, code, tables and pictures (saved in a "… pictures" folder beside the document). Downloading also writes text colours and highlights, and each picture's size and alignment. Opening a Word file also reads paragraph alignment (centred, right or justified), text colours and highlights (or shaded words), so a document goes to Word and back as it was. Its **comments** become comment threads on the same words, by whoever opened it, with each one's text and date and *— (name) (in the Word file)* saying who wrote it there. Replies in Word come in as replies in their thread, and comments marked done (resolved) come in resolved. The other way, **Download as Word** puts the document's open comment threads in as Word comments on the same words, by whoever wrote them, with their replies as Word replies in the same thread (resolved ones are left out). Its **page setup** comes too: the paper (A4, Letter, Legal or A5, whichever is nearest), portrait or landscape, margins (normal, narrow or wide, nearest), the header's and footer's text, page numbers in the footer, a watermark, columns and line numbers (see ⋮ → **Page setup**).
  - **OpenDocument text (.odt, LibreOffice Writer and others):** headings, paragraphs and their alignment, bold, italic, underline, strike, sub- and superscript, text colour and highlight, links, bulleted and numbered lists (inside one another too), checklists (as ☐/☑), quotes, code, tables (with their heading row), pictures, footnotes and page breaks, both ways; chips, equations and citations go out as their text. Opening one reads the styles LibreOffice writes, such as a paragraph styled *Heading 1* or *Title*, or a whole paragraph in a colour. **Compare documents** works with .odt files too, so a document edited in LibreOffice can be checked against the one here.
  - **Excel:** every sheet, values and formulas, bold, italic and underline, text and fill colour, alignment, number, currency and percent formats with their decimals, column widths, frozen rows and columns, and conditional formatting. Excel functions Sheets doesn't know show an error in Sheets, but are kept and written back as they were. **Comments** (Microsoft 365's threaded ones) become comment threads on the same cells, with their replies, dates and who wrote them (*— (name) (in the Excel file)*), resolved ones resolved; the old-style copies Excel writes for older versions ("[Threaded comment] Your version of Excel…") are left out, and ordinary notes stay notes. The other way, **Download as Excel** writes a spreadsheet's open comment threads on their cells as Excel comment threads, by whoever wrote them, with their replies, plus the old-style copy older Excel and LibreOffice show; a file saved again by a program that keeps only that copy still comes back as the thread. **Tables** (Insert → Table in Excel) come in as alternating colours over the same cells, with their heading and total rows and the nearest colours to their style; formulas that refer to a table by name (`Sales[Amount]`, `[@Amount]`, `Sales[[#Totals],[Amount]]`, `Sales[#All]`) are written as the cells they mean, so a table's total row (`SUBTOTAL(109, …)`) keeps working. The other way, alternating colours go out as Excel tables, in the matching table style with their heading and total rows, when their heading row holds different column names (no formulas) and they use one of the usual colour sets; the cells are painted too, so programs that don't draw table styles (LibreOffice, Numbers) still show the stripes, and the file comes back as the same alternating colours. Others are painted as before. **SUBTOTAL(function_number, range…)** is a Sheets function too: 9 sums, 1 averages, 2 counts, 3 counts what isn't empty, 4 and 5 the largest and smallest, 6 the product, 7–8 standard deviations and 10–11 variances (101–111 the same).
  - **OpenDocument spreadsheet (.ods, LibreOffice Calc and others):** every sheet, values (numbers, dates, TRUE/FALSE, text on several lines) and formulas, written the way Calc does (`[.A1:.B2]`, `[$Other.C3]`, `;` between arguments) and read back to Sheets' own, with references to tables turned into ranges; bold, italic, underline, strike, text and fill colour, alignment, wrapping, borders, number formats (number, percent, currency, date), column widths, row heights, merged cells, hidden rows and columns, notes, frozen rows and columns, and alternating colours as fills. Charts, conditional formatting, dropdowns and filters stay in Sheets (and in the Excel download). **Compare spreadsheets** works with .ods files too.
  - **PowerPoint:** every slide, text boxes at their places (size, bold, italic, colour, alignment), rectangles, rounded rectangles, ellipses, triangles, diamonds, block arrows, chevrons, stars and speech bubbles (with their text), pictures (saved in a "… pictures" folder beside the presentation), slide backgrounds and speaker notes, and charts (column, bar, stacked, line, area, scatter, pie and doughnut) with the figures saved in them, at their places — they show *Figures from PowerPoint* when selected, as they aren't tied to a spreadsheet here. **Comments** on slides (PowerPoint's older kind and the newer threaded kind) become comment threads on the same slides, with their replies, dates and who wrote them (*— (name) (in the PowerPoint file)*); resolved ones come in resolved. The other way, **Download as PowerPoint** puts a presentation's open comment threads on their slides as PowerPoint comments, by whoever wrote them, with replies as replies (LibreOffice keeps them, though it shows replies as comments of their own). **Animations** carry over too: PowerPoint's entrance effects come in as *Appear*, *Fade in* or *Fly in* on the same boxes, in the same click order (other entrance effects come in as *Fade in*), and **Download as PowerPoint** writes a slide's animations back as entrance effects on click, so the presentation builds the same way in PowerPoint. Exit and emphasis effects, other kinds of chart and SmartArt don't carry over.
  - **OpenDocument presentation (.odp, LibreOffice Impress and others):** every slide with its background colour or picture, text boxes (size, bold, italic, colour, alignment, fill, outline, a link), shapes (rectangles, rounded ones, ellipses, triangles, diamonds, block arrows, chevrons, stars, callouts) with their text, lines and arrows, tables, pictures with their alt text, turned boxes at their angle, links to web pages and to other slides, speaker notes and skipped slides, both ways. Charts go out as a table of their figures and videos as their name; transitions and animations stay in Slides (and in the PowerPoint download). Opening one also reads what Impress writes itself: notes in their placeholder, and the table rather than the picture of it Impress keeps beside it.
- **Your usual format:** **Settings → File format for downloads** chooses between **Microsoft Office** (Word, Excel, PowerPoint; the default) and **OpenDocument** (.odt, .ods, .odp, for LibreOffice and others). It's what documents, spreadsheets and presentations become when several files download from Drive as a .zip and in **Download your data**, and it comes first when you attach one to an email. Each editor's ⋮ menu still offers every kind.
- **Convert uploads:** under **Settings → File format for downloads**, tick **Convert uploads** and the Word, Excel, PowerPoint, OpenDocument and Markdown files you upload in Drive (files, folders, drag and drop) become documents, spreadsheets and presentations straight away, in the folder you uploaded to, pictures and comments included as with **Open as**. The uploaded file goes to the trash (restore it from there if you want it back). A file that can't be read stays as it came, and a note says why. Only Drive's own uploads are converted: email attachments, chat files and files saved through the network drive stay as they are.
- **E-books:** a document's ⋮ menu also has **Download as e-book (.epub)**, for reading on an e-reader (Kobo, Kindle through *Send to Kindle*, Apple Books, a phone): a chapter starts at each top-level heading, with a table of contents; pictures are in the book and footnotes are at the end of their chapter. The document's owner is named as its author. **Attach from Drive** in Mail offers it too.
- Files up to 50 MB can be converted.

## Working with files (Drive)

- **Suggested:** the top of **My Drive** shows a few files you're likely to want next, each saying why: attached to a meeting of yours in the next day, repeating ones included (*For “Design review” at 2:00 PM*), changed by someone else this week (*Kim Park changed it today*), or opened by you lately. Only files you can open; **▾ Suggested** hides them (remembered in this browser).
- **Folder sizes:** a folder's **Size** is what everything inside it adds up to, at any depth (hover for how many files), or *Empty*; the grid shows it too, and sorting by size goes by it. Files in the trash aren't counted.
- **Sorting and grid:** click **Name**, **Modified** or **Size** at the top of the list to sort by it (again for the other way round; newest and largest come first to start with). Folders always stay first, and numbers in names count as numbers (*Report 2* before *Report 10*). The buttons at the top right switch between the list and a **grid** of tiles, with a small picture of each photo and a **Sort by** menu; ticking, dragging onto folders, double-clicking to open and the ⋮ menu work the same. Your choice is kept for you on the server, so it's the same on every computer. Recent stays newest first, and search results best match first, until you sort them.
- **Stars:** ⋮ → *Add star* on anything you want to find again; **Starred** in the sidebar lists them. Stars are your own.
- **Chat with people who have access:** a file's or folder's menu (and ⋮ in a document, spreadsheet or presentation) has **Chat with people who have access**: a private Chat channel for it with its owner and the colleagues it's shared with by name (directly or through a folder above, while their access lasts; not company-wide or group access), its link posted first. It's made the first time; after that it's the same channel, with anyone given access since added. Not yet shared, it says so.
- **What a channel is about:** channels made this way (for an event, a task list, a file or folder, or a note) say so at the top instead of a topic: *📅 Launch review · this channel's event*, a link that opens it (kept up to date if it's renamed, the next time someone opens its chat). If it's deleted or in the trash, the top of the channel says so instead of linking to it; the conversation stays.
- **Several at once:** tick files and folders (or Ctrl/Shift-click a row, or Space on it) and use the bar above the list to download, star, move or trash them together, or **Copy links** (each one's name and link on a line of its own, to paste in an email or a chat; a document opens in its editor, a folder in Drive, and an uploaded file's link opens Drive with that file showing; only people who can open them get in). One item's **⋮ → Copy link** does the same for it alone. **Send in Chat…** puts links to them in a conversation's message box (after anything you'd typed there) and opens it, to add a word and send; they show as file cards, and anyone there who can't open them is offered access. **Email links** starts a new email listing them as links; when you send it, Mail offers to share any a recipient can't open yet. The box in the header ticks everything.
- **Rename several:** with two or more ticked, **Rename** in that bar renames them together: **Find** and **Replace with** (any case, or **Match case**), and text to **Add before** or **Add after** (after goes before a file's extension: *report.pdf* → *report (final).pdf*). **New name for all** replaces each name (keeping a file's extension) and **Number them** adds 1, 2, 3… (from **Start at**) in the names' order (*img2* before *img10*), with as many digits as the last number needs: ticked holiday photos become *Holiday 01.jpg* … *Holiday 12.jpg*. A table shows each new name as you type; only the ones you can edit are renamed, and a name that's taken in the same folder gets a number. Each rename is in the file's activity. **Undo** on the *Renamed N* message right after puts every name back as it was. **Make a copy** (two or more ticked, folders too) puts a copy of each next to it (*Copy of …*), checking first that there's room for all of them; files you can't copy (downloads turned off by their owner) are skipped with a note. Moving (with **Move** or by dragging), **Trash** and **Make a copy** have **Undo** on their message too: everything moved goes back to the folder it came from (or My Drive), what was trashed comes back out of the trash, and the copies just made go to the trash.
- **Drag to move:** drag files onto a folder in the list, or onto a folder in the path at the top, to move them there. Dragging one of several ticked files moves them all.
- **Shortcuts:** **⋮ → Add shortcut to Drive…** on any file or folder you can open (yours or shared with you) puts a shortcut to it in My Drive or a folder you choose, marked with ↪, without moving or copying it: open it from there, and **Show file location** goes to where it really is. In a shared folder, everyone who can open what it points to sees the shortcut. If it's no longer shared with you, only you see the shortcut, marked as such, so you can **Remove shortcut** (removing one never touches the file itself).
- **My templates:** **⋮ → Use as my template** on a document, spreadsheet or presentation you own makes it one of your own templates: **New → From a template** then lists it under **My templates** (above your company's and the ready-made ones), and picking it starts a new copy, leaving the original as it is. Only you see your templates; **Remove from my templates** takes it off the list (the file stays). Company templates, made by admins, are for everyone in the company.
- **Labels:** **⋮ → Labels…** on a file or folder (yours or shared with you) ticks labels of your own on it, or adds a new one (*Invoices*, *Taxes 2026*; up to 10 on each). They show under its name, and **Labels** on the left lists each with how many files have it: click one to see them all, wherever they are. Labels are yours alone (others label the same file their own way); files in the trash leave the counts. The ⋯ beside a label on the left picks a **colour** for it (a dot on the left, and its chips on files take the colour; your own, and it goes with a rename), and has **Rename label…** (on all your files with it; a name you already use joins the two) and **Delete label** (it comes off your files; the files stay). To label several at once, tick them and use **🏷 Label…** in the bar above the list (their other labels stay).
- **Make a copy** (⋮ menu) of a file, document, spreadsheet, presentation or folder; it goes next to the original (or in My Drive if you can't add to that folder), with **Undo** on its message. A folder's copy has everything in it, sub-folders and all (up to 2,000 items), keeping its colour; what's in the trash isn't copied, and anything inside you can't copy (downloads turned off by its owner) is left out, with a note saying how many. A shared drive itself can't be copied, but the folders in it can. **Copy to…** (⋮ menu, or **Copy to** with several ticked) puts the copies in a folder you pick instead; there they keep their names (a number is added if one's taken), with **Undo** too.
- **PDF tools:** tick PDFs (and PNG or JPEG pictures, such as scans or a signature) and **Combine into PDF** makes one PDF of them, in the order you set with ↑ ↓ (each picture gets a page of its own, fitted on an A4 page), named as you like, next to the first file. A PDF's ⋮ menu has **Extract pages…**: list pages and ranges (*1-3, 5, 8-*), in any order, optionally turned a quarter or half turn, and they go into a new PDF beside it. The originals are never changed, and files whose owner turned off downloading can't be used. Done on the server, nothing is sent elsewhere.
- **Organise a PDF's pages:** ⋮ → **Organise pages…** shows them as thumbnails: drag one to a new place (or ← →), **↻** turns it a quarter turn, **×** takes it out. **Save** changes the PDF itself (for its editors; the pages as they were stay in **Version history**), or **Save as a copy** makes *… (reorganised).pdf* beside it.
- **Fill & sign a PDF:** a PDF's ⋮ menu → **Fill & sign…** shows its pages; choose **✍ Signature** (draw it with the mouse, a pen or a finger, or type your name in a handwriting style), **📅 Date** (today), **T Text** or **✓ Tick**, then click where it goes. Drag things into place, × removes one. **Save signed copy** makes *… (signed).pdf* beside it (the original stays as it was), with everything exactly where you put it, on pages turned sideways too. Works for any PDF you can download, including ones shared with you.
- **Email as attachment:** with this server's email on, a file's **⋮ → Email as attachment** opens a new message in Mail with the file attached and its name as the subject. A document, spreadsheet or presentation asks first what to attach it as (Word, Excel, PowerPoint, OpenDocument, Markdown, or a link to it), as **Attach from Drive** does. Files shared with you can be sent too, unless their owner has turned off downloading.
- **Zip files:** opening a .zip in Drive lists what's inside (folders indented, each file's size, and the total unpacked). **Extract here** unpacks it into a new folder of the same name next to it (or in your Drive if you can't add to that folder), with its folders as they were. Junk such as `__MACOSX` and `.DS_Store` is left out, names can't climb out of the new folder, and it counts towards the company's storage. Up to 5,000 files and 2 GB unpacked; password-protected or damaged zips, and zips that unpack to more than they say, are refused and nothing is left behind. People who can only view a view-only zip can look inside but not unpack it.
- **Scan to PDF:** **New → Scan to PDF…** turns photos of paper (receipts, signed forms, whiteboard notes) into one PDF in the folder you're in. On a phone, **Take a photo** opens the camera, a page at a time; **Choose pictures** picks ones you already took. Put the pages in order (‹ ›) or remove one, keep **Black and white, like a scanner** (paper white, ink black, small files) or untick it for colour, name it and **Save PDF**. Each page is A4 wide, in the photo's proportions; it's all done on your device and then uploaded like any file.
- **Download as .zip:** a folder's ⋮ menu, or **Download** with several ticked, gives one .zip with the folders inside it. Documents, spreadsheets and presentations go in as Word, Excel and PowerPoint files. Up to 2 GB at a time.
- **Trash:** what you trash waits in **Trash**, where each item says when it goes for good: after 30 days unless an admin changes it (Admin → Company settings → **Empty the trash automatically**: 7 days to a year, or never). Its versions go with it and the space is freed. **Restore** brings it back and stops the countdown; **Empty trash** clears it at once.
