Each list says **Not listed**, **Listed** (with **Ask to be removed**, to use once the cause is fixed), or **Couldn't check**: Spamhaus won't answer lookups made through big public DNS services, so set `MAIL_DNS_SERVERS` to your own resolver (your router or ISP's) for it. It's also one of the health checks in **Admin → System**, looked up again every few hours: when the address lands on a list, the platform owners get a notification (*Needs attention: Spam blocklists*), as for the other checks.
- **Email log** (Admin → Email): find out what happened to a message sent from or to your domains. Search by an address or Message-ID, what happened and how far back; each recipient shows **Delivered**, **Waiting to retry** (with how many tries), **Bounced** or **Refused** (turned away when it arrived: an address that doesn't exist, spam…), with what the receiving server said (*250 OK*, *550 5.1.1 User unknown*, *No route to host*). Mail sent to an alias or group shows who it reached. Admins see their own company's domains only. It comes from the mail server's own log, which the mail container now writes to its status folder (kept to about 60 MB: the current file and the two before it), and still shows on the container's console.
  - Real mailboxes and aliases keep their own mail.
  - The catch-all must be a mailbox in the same company, so the server can't be used to relay a domain's junk elsewhere.
  - Catch-alls attract extra spam; turn it off to return such mail to the sender again.
- **Mailbox size limits** (Admin → Users → ⋮ → Mailbox size). When a mailbox is full, new mail bounces to the sender.
- **Emptying Trash and Junk:** Admin → Company settings → **Emptying Trash and Junk** has the mail server delete mail that has been in Trash, or in Junk, for a while (7, 14, 30, 60 or 90 days, or a year; *Never* by default), for everyone's mailbox in the company, whether it's read in Workspace, on a phone or in Outlook. It frees the space it took.
- **Spam:** Rspamd scores incoming mail, rejects obvious spam and files likely spam in Junk.
- **Suspended users:** disabling a user blocks sign-in everywhere, but their mail keeps arriving.
- **Drafts:** compose saves to the Drafts folder 2 seconds after you stop typing. Closing the window keeps the draft, the bin icon discards it, and clicking a draft in Drafts resumes it.
  - Drafts live on the mail server, so phones and Outlook see them.
  - Attachments in a draft are kept as links to Drive files. They're restored when the draft is reopened in webmail, but phones don't see them until the message is sent.
- **Drag and drop:** drag a message from the list onto a folder in the sidebar to move it there (Archive, Junk and Trash too), or onto a label to put that label on it. When it's one of several ticked messages, they all go. The folder or label lights up while you're over it, and the move has **Undo**.
- **Reading pane:** **Settings → Mail list and reading pane** puts the email you open **right of the list** (the usual), **below the list** (the list across the top, the email under it), or **no split**: the list fills the page, and an email opens in its place with ← back to the list. Kept with your account. Phones always show one at a time. Beside the list, **drag the line between the list and the email** (or focus it and use ← →) to make the list wider or narrower (260 to 800 pixels, and never more than about half the page); it's kept with your account, and **double-click** it for the usual width. The same settings have **Show sender pictures in the list** (a colleague's photo, or the sender's initials; in Sent and Drafts, the first person it went to) and **Compact list** (sender, subject and date on one line, so more fit on the screen).
- **Undo:** archiving, deleting (to Trash), reporting spam (or *Not spam*) and moving to a folder show **Undo** in the message at the bottom for a few seconds, for one message or several picked at once: it puts them back in the folder they came from, read or unread and starred as before. Deleting from Trash or Junk is for good, so there's no Undo there.
- **Undo send:** after **Send**, the message waits five seconds with an **Undo** button, in case you spot a mistake; Undo brings the message back as it was. (Leaving the page in those seconds sends it straight away.)
- **After archiving or deleting:** Settings → **After archiving or deleting an email** chooses what opens once the message you're reading is archived, deleted, moved, reported as spam or snoozed: **the list** (as before), **the next newer message** or **the next older one** (back to the list when there's none that way). Kept with your account.
- **Send & archive:** when replying (Reply or Reply all), **Send & archive** next to **Send** sends your message and moves the one you answered out of the Inbox into **Archive**, so a finished conversation doesn't wait in the Inbox. It can be undone like any send; the toast says *Sent … and archived*. Replies to something already in Archive just have **Send**.
- **Forgot the attachment?** If what you wrote says a file comes with it (*I've attached*, *see attachment*, *enclosed*, *PFA*, *please find the invoice*, or the subject says so) but nothing is attached, **Send** (and **Send later**) first asks *Did you mean to attach something?*: **Attach a file** opens the file picker, **Send anyway** sends it as it is. What's quoted from the message you're replying to doesn't count (it may well have had the file), nor does the subject of a reply or forward, and a forwarded message's attachments or a link to a Drive file count as attached. *Attached to the idea* isn't a file. **Settings → Mail → Forgotten attachments** turns the question off, or adds words of your own that mean a file comes with it (another language's, *Anlage*, *bilaga*, or your own *spec sheet*; up to 20, matched as whole words in any case).
- **Include Kim?** As you put people on a message, a line under the addresses suggests up to three others you usually write to together with them (*Include Lee Chen · Max Moss ?*): someone who was on at least half of what you sent to them, at least twice. Click a name to add them to **To**, or **×** to stop suggesting them for this message. It learns from what you send from Workspace (not from mail sent before this, or from other mail apps), it's yours alone, and messages to more than 20 people don't count. Not for shared mailboxes.
- **Going outside the company:** when anyone on a message (To, Cc or Bcc) isn't on one of your company's own domains, a notice under the addresses says so: *Going outside Acme Trading: pat@partner.example*, or *3 recipients outside …* with each address. Those you've never written to from Workspace and don't have in your contacts are marked **(new)**, so a mistyped or unexpected address stands out before you send. It's a notice, not a question, unless the company wants one: **Admin → Company settings → Mail going outside the company → Ask before sending a message to anyone outside the company** makes **Send** (and **Send later**) ask *Send outside Acme Trading?* with the addresses (and which are new); **Go back** leaves the message open, **Send** sends it. The server holds such a message back until the sender says yes, whichever way it's sent from Workspace. Each one sent (or scheduled) after saying yes goes in **Admin → Activity** as *Sent an email outside the company (after being asked)*, with its subject and the outside addresses. Mail between colleagues goes as usual, and Admin → Data protection can still stop or warn about what goes out.
- **After their working hours:** when a colleague on a message has set working hours (Calendar → Working hours) and their day is over, a note under the addresses says so with their local time (*🌙 It's 23:10 for Kim Park, outside their working hours*), and **Send when their day starts (…)** schedules the message for then, as **Send later** would (with several such colleagues, for when the last of them starts). Sending now still works as usual.
- **Away:** when a colleague on a message has their **Vacation reply** on today, a note under the addresses says so before you send: *🌴 Lee Chen is away until 17 Nov (“At the trade fair”)*, with their reply's subject (no end date: just *is away*). It's for colleagues in your company only, and goes when they turn it off.
- **Full mailbox, many people:** when a colleague on a message has a full mailbox (as the hourly check last found), a note says your message will come back from them until they make room. A message to 25 people or more says how many it goes to (*👥 This goes to 32 people*), so a big group or reply all doesn't go by unnoticed. **Settings → Mail → Tips while writing** turns all these notes off (people to include, after their working hours, away, full mailbox, many people, outside the company); a company's *ask before sending outside* still asks.
- **Follow-up reminders:** the **⏰** list in the message window (*No reply in 1 day*, *2 days*, *3 days*, *a week* or *2 weeks*) asks Workspace to check on it then: if nobody has replied (a reply anywhere in your folders except Sent, Drafts, Junk and Trash counts), you get a notification — *No reply yet: “Subject”* — that opens the message. The message in Sent shows when you'll be reminded, with **Cancel reminder**. Home's **Waiting for a reply** card lists them all. Forgot to ask when sending? Open the message in **Sent** and choose **⏰ Remind me if no reply…** there (counted from then). It works with scheduled messages too (counted from when they go out), and can be turned off in the bell's settings.
- **Schedule send:** the clock button next to Send sends it later: tomorrow morning or afternoon, Monday morning, or a date and time you pick. The server sends it then, even if nobody has Workspace open, with the time it was sent as its date. Waiting messages are listed under **Scheduled** in the folder list, with **Send now** and **Don't send** (which puts it back in Drafts to change). The message is put together when you schedule it, so attachments are as they were then. If sending fails three times it stays in the list with the reason.
- **Add to contacts:** next to the sender of a message from outside your company, **Add to contacts** saves them (name and address) to your contacts; once they're there it says *In your contacts*.
- **Warnings on mail from outside:** **Admin → Company settings → Mark email from outside the company** puts a note above every email from an address that isn't on the company's domains: *From outside Acme Trading (gina@globex.com): be careful with links, attachments and requests for money or passwords.* Whether that's on or not, an email from outside that uses the name of someone who works at the company (*"Olivia Owner" <boss@globex.com>*, a common trick to get money sent) always gets a red warning: *Careful: this may be someone pretending to be Olivia Owner*, with the real address. Names are compared ignoring capitals, spaces and quotes; suspended colleagues don't count.
- **Report phishing:** on a message, **⚠ Report phishing** (after you confirm) moves it to Junk (with **Undo**) and tells your company's admins at once, in their bell: *Kim Park reported a phishing email from payroll@evilpay.com*, with the subject and how many people have reported that sender. **Admin → Phishing** lists the reports by sender (how many, who, the subjects, when), the ones to look at first; **Mark handled** once you've warned people and dealt with it (a new report brings it back). **Block sender** or **Block domain** there (or type an address or domain under **Blocked for everyone**) sends their future mail straight to Junk, marked read, for everyone in the company, on phones too, before anyone's filters, forwarding or vacation reply (a domain covers its subdomains; your own company's addresses can't be blocked this way). Blocking handles the sender's reports, **Unblock** lets their mail through again, and both are in the activity log. **Warn everyone** on a report puts a warning at the top of Workspace for everyone for a week, already written (*⚠ Phishing: emails from payroll@evilpay.com (“Confirm your bank details”) are a scam…*) and yours to change first; Admin → Announcements ends it early. **Remove from inboxes** on a report searches everyone's Inbox in the company for mail from that sender and moves it to Junk, marked read (on phones too), even mail nobody has opened yet; nothing is deleted, and it says how many emails moved from how many people (and any mailbox it couldn't reach). The sender and subject are read from the mail server, not taken from the browser, and reporting the same email twice counts once.
- **Block sender:** on a message, **Block sender** (after you confirm) makes a filter that sends their future mail straight to Junk, marked read (on your phone too, since filters run on the mail server), and moves the message there now. It shows in **Filters** as *Blocked: their address*, where deleting it unblocks them.
- **Mark all as read:** when a folder has unread mail, the ✓ button above the list marks all of it as read at once (on the server, so your phone sees it too).
- **Unsubscribe:** newsletters and mailing lists that say how to leave (the List-Unsubscribe header) show **Unsubscribe** beside the sender. After you confirm, Workspace asks the list to stop: with a one-click request when the list supports it (RFC 8058), or else by sending the list's unsubscribe email from your address. Lists that only offer a web page open it in a new tab. For safety, the server only contacts https addresses on the public internet, never your own network, and doesn't follow redirects.
- **Manage subscriptions:** **Manage subscriptions** in the Mail sidebar lists every newsletter and mailing list in your Inbox from the last six months, by sender, the busiest first, with how many emails each sent. **Unsubscribe** leaves (in one click where the sender allows it, or by sending their unsubscribe email from you; a few only offer a page, which opens); **Archive all** clears that sender's mail out of your Inbox.
- **Invitations from other calendars:** an email with an invitation from Outlook, Gmail, Apple Calendar or another calendar shows it as a card above the message: what, when, where and who's organising it, with **Yes**, **Maybe** and **No**. Yes or Maybe puts it on your calendar (marked as an invitation from them, and synced to your phone like your other events) and emails the organiser your answer the standard way, so their calendar shows it; No tells them and takes it off again. You can change your mind with the same buttons; **See it in Calendar** opens it there. A cancellation shows as *Cancelled* with **Remove from my calendar**. Invitations from colleagues on this server are answered in Calendar (**Answer in Calendar**).
- **Answers to your invitations by email:** when a guest answers from their own calendar app (Accept in Outlook, Yes in Gmail, a phone), the answer email they send back goes on your event by itself: their Yes, Maybe or No shows in the guest list, you get a notification (*Ann accepted “Contract review”*), and the email shows a card saying so with **See it in Calendar**. It's recorded as it arrives while you have Workspace open, or else when you open the email. Guests can still answer on the page linked in the invitation too.
- **Delegation (an assistant):** **Settings → Mail delegation** gives colleagues at your company access to your own mail (up to 10): they pick it under **Mailbox** in Mail, read and organise it, and write or reply as you. What they send comes from your address, with a *Sender:* line naming them, so the recipient's mail app (and Workspace, as *sent by …*) shows who actually wrote it; it's kept in your Sent. Your filters, signatures, labels, settings and scheduled mail stay yours, and they can't pass access on. They get a notification when given access; **Remove** ends it at once. Giving and removing access are in the activity log.
- **Shared mailboxes:** an admin adds one in **Admin → Email → Shared mailboxes** (an address such as *support@* or *info@* on a company domain, a name for its mail, and the people who use it). It has its own mailbox: those people pick it under **Mailbox** at the top of the Mail sidebar, read its folders, move, flag and delete, and **reply or write as it** (the From is the shared address, and what's sent is kept in its Sent, so everyone sees what was answered). Nobody signs in to it, and it isn't in anyone's own Inbox. Filters, labels, snoozing, scheduled sending, follow-ups and signatures stay with each person's own mail. Taking someone off the list closes it to them at once. **Who answers what:** in a shared mailbox, an open message has **Given to** (one of its people, or **Take it** for yourself; they get a notification, which they can turn off in the bell's settings) and **Mark done**. The list shows who each message is given to and which are done, and **All / Given to me / Not done** above it narrows the page; everyone using the mailbox sees changes as they're made. An admin can also turn on an **automatic reply** for it (*Thanks, we got your message…*, with its own subject), sent from the shared address at most once a day to each sender. It can have its own **signature** (*{name}* becomes whoever writes, so a reply ends *Kim Park · Acme Support*), added to new mail and replies written as it instead of your own. **Internal notes** under an open message are a short discussion for the mailbox's people only (*Customer is on the gold plan*, *Refund approved*); they're never sent, the list shows 💬 with how many, and whoever wrote a note can delete it. The **Mailbox** picker shows how much is unread in each shared mailbox's Inbox (checked every minute), and while you're in your own mail a line under it says which shared mailboxes have unread mail.
- **Plus addresses:** mail sent to *you+anything@yourcompany* reaches you (handy for sign-ups: *kim+shop@…*). In **Filters**, tick **Put mail sent to you+receipts@… into my folder called receipts** and each plus address goes into your folder of that name (*receipts*, *Receipts*), so mail sorts itself as it arrives, on phones too. Only folders you already have are used, so a sender can't make new ones: a name with no folder goes to the Inbox.
- **Mute:** **Mute** on an open message stops a busy conversation (a big reply-all thread, say) from filling your Inbox: it's archived, and from then on replies in it go straight to **Archive**, already read, without notifying you. It works on the mail server, so phones and mail apps see the same. A muted conversation's messages say so, with **Unmute**; **Filters** lists your muted conversations to unmute them there.
- **Confidential mode:** in a message, choose **🔒 Confidential** and how long it can be read (a day, a week, a month or three months). The message itself stays on this server: the email that goes out only says who sent it, the subject and a link. Colleagues it was sent to open the link signed in; anyone else types their address and gets a code by email (it works once, for 10 minutes). The page has no forward, download or print (that stops casual sharing, not a photo of the screen). **Confidential messages** in the Mail sidebar lists yours (to whom, until when, how often opened) and **Take back** stops the link at once. Confidential messages can't carry attachments (share the files from Drive instead) or be scheduled.
- **Storage:** **Storage** in the Mail sidebar shows how much room your mail takes: the total (against your mailbox size limit, with a bar and a warning when it's nearly full, if an admin set one), each folder's size and number of messages, and your 25 largest messages (over 512 KB) with who sent them, where they are and how big they are. **Open** goes to one; the bin deletes it (to Trash, which still counts until it's emptied, or for good from Trash). Mail on phones and in Outlook counts too.
- **Mailbox nearly full:** with a mailbox size limit, you get a notification (in the bell, and on your devices if they have notifications on) when your mail reaches 90% of it (*Your mailbox is 93% full*), and again when it's full and new mail starts going back to the sender. Each comes once, and again only after you've made room below 90%. It's checked every hour, and straight away when an admin changes your limit. Clicking it opens **Storage**. It can be turned off with the bell's ⚙ (*Your mailbox filling up*).
- **Emptying Trash and Junk yourself:** in **Trash** or **Junk**, **Empty Trash now** (or *Empty Junk now*) above the list deletes everything in it for good, after asking; **Storage** has an **Empty** beside them too, saying how much room it freed. Only those two folders can be emptied this way, and not while your company keeps your mail (a legal hold). Admins can also have them emptied automatically after so many days (below).
- **Tidy the inbox:** **Settings → Tidy the inbox** moves mail in the **Updates** tab (newsletters, notifications, receipts) to **Archive** by itself once it's a week, 2 weeks, 30, 60 or 90 days old (*Never* by default). It runs once a day, on the mail server, so it covers mail read on phones and in Outlook too; **Tidy now** does it straight away. Starred mail stays put, and so does anything moved to Primary by hand. Nothing is deleted, and archived mail is still found by search.
- **Inbox tabs:** the Inbox is split into **Primary** (mail from people) and **Updates** (newsletters, notifications, receipts and mailing lists: mail that says it's sent to a list, has an unsubscribe link, or was sent automatically; an automatic *reply* to you, such as a colleague's out-of-office answer, stays in Primary). The other tab shows how many unread it has. Picked the wrong one? Tick messages and **Move to Primary** (or **Move to Updates**): they stay where you put them. Searching looks through the whole Inbox, and other mail apps still see one Inbox. **Filters → Split the Inbox** turns the tabs off.
- **Saved searches:** after a search, **Save this search** keeps it under **Searches** in the Mail sidebar with how many unread messages match; click it to run it again, × to remove it.
- **Conversations:** above a message, **Conversation: 2 earlier, 1 later messages** lists the rest of its conversation, oldest first: messages with the same subject (ignoring Re:, Fwd: and the like, in several languages) that share someone with it, from the Inbox, Sent, Archive and the folder you're in, so your own replies are there too. Click one to open it.
- **Import mail:** **Import mail** in the Mail sidebar copies everything from another account (Gmail, Outlook.com, Yahoo, iCloud or any IMAP server) into your mailbox: every folder, with what's read and starred and the original dates. Sent, Drafts and Archive go to yours, and Junk and Trash are left out. It runs on the server with progress shown (you can close the window), and messages already here are skipped, so it can be run again. Your password there is used only for the import and never stored. Gmail, Yahoo and iCloud need an app password. Only servers on the internet are allowed, on ports 993 or 143; set `MAIL_IMPORT_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1` to allow an old mail server on your own network. **From an .mbox file:** the same window takes an .mbox file (Gmail's Takeout, Thunderbird, Apple Mail, or Workspace's own *Download your data*): **Upload an .mbox file…** (it's kept in your Drive) or **Choose from Drive…**. Its messages go into a folder named after the file (*Old account.mbox* into *Old account*; *Inbox.mbox*, *Sent.mbox*, *Drafts.mbox* and *Archive.mbox* into those folders), with their own dates, read unless the file says otherwise, and starred when it says so (Thunderbird's status, Gmail's *Unread* and *Starred* labels). Messages already in that folder are skipped, so importing the same file again adds nothing. Progress shows as for an account, and it carries on if you close the window.
- **Saved emails (.eml):** a folder's **⋮ → Upload emails (.eml or .zip)…** (Inbox, Sent, Archive, Junk, Trash or your own folders; not Drafts) puts emails saved as .eml files (from Outlook, Thunderbird, Apple Mail, or **Download** here) into that folder, several at once: each keeps its own date, attachments and headers, and comes in read unless the file says it wasn't (a *Status* header without R). One that's already in the folder (the same Message-ID) is skipped, so nothing doubles; a file that isn't an email is named and left out. Up to 30 MB each. A **.zip of .eml files** (such as one made by **Download (.zip)** below) goes in whole: every email in it, in any folder inside it, with ones already there skipped and files that aren't emails listed (up to 2,000 emails or 500 MB). You can also drag .eml files (or such a .zip) from your computer onto a folder in the sidebar, or onto the message list to put them in the folder you're looking at (other kinds of files are refused).
- **Print several at once:** tick messages and **Print** puts them on one printout (or PDF), each starting a new page with its sender, recipients, date and subject, the same as printing one; up to 50 at a time, and unread ones stay unread.
- **Download several at once:** tick messages in the list and **Download (.zip)** gives one .zip with each as an .eml file, named by its date and subject (*2026-11-03 Quarterly numbers.eml*), with its attachments and headers whole: for keeping, for another mail program, or to upload again here. Up to 500 messages (200 MB) at a time; in a shared mailbox too. A folder's **⋮ → Download folder (.zip)** does the same for everything in it, oldest first (up to 2,000 messages, 500 MB; for more, use **Settings → Download your data**, which gives every folder as .mbox). Both are checked first, so if it can't be done (the folder was emptied meanwhile, or it's too big) a message says why instead of a file being saved.
- **Linked files:** if an email links to Drive files that colleagues it's going to can't open, Mail asks before sending: **Share (can view) and send** shares the ones that are yours, **Send anyway** sends it as it is. Files that aren't yours are named so you can ask their owner. People outside the company aren't checked.
- **Delivered?** Open a message in **Sent** and a line under its header says whether it reached everyone: **✓ Delivered to …**, or which recipients are still waiting (the receiving server didn't answer: Workspace's mail server keeps trying for up to 5 days) or weren't delivered, with what the receiving server said. Mail to a group or alias shows who it reached. It comes from the mail server's log (see *Email log*), so it's there for mail sent since that log started, and only for the person who sent it.
- **Read receipts:** tick **Read receipt** when writing to ask to be told when it's opened (the recipient's mail app may ask them, and they can say no). When a message you open asks for one, a bar offers **Send receipt** or **Not now**; Workspace never sends one by itself, and either answer is remembered so you aren't asked again (in phone mail apps too). The receipt only says the message was opened.
- **Quoted history folded away:** a reply shows just what's new; the earlier messages quoted under it (from Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, Thunderbird, Workspace, or `>` lines in plain text) are folded behind a **•••** button that shows them, and hides them again. Only a quote at the end is folded, so answers written in between quoted lines stay as they are, and a message that's all quote is shown whole.
- **Looking at attachments:** click a picture, PDF, text file, audio or video attachment, a Word (.docx), Excel (.xlsx) or PowerPoint (.pptx) file, an OpenDocument one (.odt, .ods, .odp), or a .zip (to see what's inside it; save it to Drive to unpack it), to open it in Workspace instead of downloading it. Office files are shown read-only, the way Docs, Sheets and Slides show them (formulas worked out, pictures included), and nothing is saved anywhere. **←** and **→** (or the arrow keys) go through the others that can be shown ("2 of 3"), and the viewer has **Download** and **Save to Drive**. Other kinds (older .doc or .xls files, or .rar and .7z archives, for example) download as before, and Shift+click downloads anything. Attachments come from anyone, so only kinds that can't run code are shown: an SVG picture or a web page always downloads, the kind is taken from the file's name rather than what the sender says, and what's shown is locked down like Drive's previews.
- **Attached emails:** an email attached to another (a .eml file, such as one sent with **Forward as attachment**) opens in the viewer as an email: who it's from and to, the date, the subject, the text, and the names and sizes of its own attachments. It's shown as plain text, so nothing in it runs or loads. A .eml file in Drive, or one shared in Chat, opens the same way.
- **Large files as links:** messages can carry up to 25 MB of attachments. Beyond that, **Send the large files as links** (under the attachments) ticks **As a link** on your own files of 1 MB or more (or tick it on any of yours): they go out as download links at the end of the message instead (*Files (download links, until …)*, with names and sizes), each a public link that lasts 30 days, so people outside the company can download them too. The links show in the file's Share window, where you can remove them early, and making them is in the activity log. Files others shared with you can only be attached; and when the company has turned public links off, nothing can be sent as a link.
- **Attaching documents, spreadsheets and presentations:** **Attach from Drive** in a message offers your Workspace documents, spreadsheets and presentations too. Pick one and choose what it goes as: a document as **Word (.docx)**, **OpenDocument (.odt)** or **Markdown (.md)**, a spreadsheet as **Excel (.xlsx)** or **OpenDocument (.ods)**, a presentation as **PowerPoint (.pptx)** or **OpenDocument (.odp)**. Or choose **A link to it** to put a named link in the message instead (📄 *Project brief*): recipients open it in Workspace and always see the latest version, and if some of them can't open it, Mail offers to share it when you send. A copy as it is at that moment is attached (later changes aren't in it) and kept in your **Email attachments** folder; attaching the same one again updates that copy (the earlier one stays in its version history), so the attachment keeps its plain name. Not possible for a file whose owner turned off downloading for people who can only view it.
- **Download all:** a message with two or more attachments has **Download all (.zip)** beside them: one .zip named after the subject (*Supplier quotes (attachments).zip*), with each file as it came, under a name that's safe on any computer (two with the same name are numbered).
- **Save to Drive:** the folder button beside an attachment saves it to Drive, and **Save all to Drive** (when there are several) saves them all, into an **Email attachments** folder in My Drive (made the first time). **Show** in the note opens the folder. Saved files count towards the company's storage.
- **Create event:** the calendar button on a message opens a new event in Calendar with the subject (without Re: or Fwd:), everyone on the email except you as guests, and its text as the description. It starts at the first hour you and the colleagues on the email are all free (within working hours; people outside the company can't be checked, so with none it's the next half hour). Change the time if you like and **Save**; nothing is sent until you do.
- **Search:** the search box finds messages in the folder you're in with all the words you type (in the subject, sender, recipients or text). Like Gmail, it also understands `"exact phrases"`, `-word` to leave out, `from:` `to:` `subject:`, `has:attachment`, `is:unread` `is:read` `is:starred`, `before:2026-01-31` `after:2026-01-01`, `newer_than:7d` `older_than:1y` (days, months, years), and `larger:5M` `smaller:200K`. A mistake (a date written another way) is explained instead of finding nothing. Hover over the box for the list.
- **Print:** the printer button on a message opens your browser's print dialog with a clean page: the subject, From, To, Cc and date, the attachments' names, and the message (pictures from the internet only if you chose to show them). Choose *Save as PDF* there to keep a copy.
- **Labels:** **+** next to Labels in the folder list makes a coloured label. Put it on a message with the tag button (or **Label** with several selected); take it off with the × on its chip. Click a label to see only the messages that have it in the folder you're in. Labels are kept on the mail server as IMAP keywords, so mail apps that show keywords (such as Thunderbird's tags) see them; deleting a label takes it off every message in every folder.
- **Templates:** for messages you send often, write one and choose **Templates → Save as template…** in the message window. Later, **Templates → (its name)** puts its text where the cursor is (in place of an empty message) and its subject if there isn't one yet; your signature stays as it is. **Manage templates…** renames them, changes their subject or deletes them.
- **Moving templates:** **Templates → Manage templates…** (or **Import templates…** when you have none) has **Export**, which saves them all as *templates.json*, and **Import…**, which reads one back here or on another Workspace server: ones already here are skipped, and one whose name is taken by a different template gets a number.
- **Snooze:** the clock button on a message (or **Snooze** with several selected) puts it away until later today, tomorrow, the weekend, next week, or a date and time you pick (up to a year ahead). It waits in a **Snoozed** folder, with when it comes back, and returns to the top of your Inbox, unread, at that time, even if nobody has Workspace open. **Unsnooze** brings it back now; snoozing it again changes the time. Phones and Outlook see the Snoozed folder too; a message moved out of it by hand stays where it's put.
- **Keyboard:** **C** writes a new message, **/** searches, **J** / **K** open the next older or newer message, **U** goes back to the list, **R** / **A** / **F** reply, reply all or forward, **E** archives, **#** deletes, **S** stars, **Shift+U** marks unread, **V** moves to a folder and **L** puts on a label (the open message, or the ticked ones: type part of the name, ↑/↓ to choose, Enter to pick, and Undo after a move). They work whenever you aren't typing in a box; **?** (or Ctrl+/) lists them.
- **Rich-text writing:** bold, italic, underline, strike, bulleted and numbered lists, quotes, links and tables. Every message is sent with a formatted version and a readable plain-text version (quotes marked with `>`, lists with `-` / `1.`), so any mail app can show it. Replies quote the original as a quoted block. Quotes are built from the original's plain text, so formatting tricks in a received email can't carry into yours.
- **Alignment:** the alignment button's **Left / Centre / Right** align the paragraph the cursor is in (or every selected paragraph). Keyboard: Ctrl+Shift+L / E / R. Mail apps show the alignment as sent.
  - **Pictures:** click a picture, then use the same alignment button to put it on the left, in the centre or on the right. Drafts keep it, and the email is built so Outlook on Windows shows it the same way.
- **Toolbar:** one compact row in a normal window (it wraps on phones). From left to right:
  - **Undo / Redo** (also Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Y). Each is greyed out when there's nothing to undo or redo.
  - Font and size, **B I U**, text and highlight colour.
  - An **alignment** button that shows the current alignment and opens Left / Centre / Right.
  - Lists, link, picture, **emoji** and table.
  - **⋮ More formatting**, which holds Strikethrough, Quote, Decrease / Increase indent and Clear formatting.
  - The menus work from the keyboard: Enter or ↓ opens, the arrow keys move, Enter picks, and Escape closes the menu (not the message). Choices that can be on or off show as checked.
  - Picture and table tools appear only while a picture or table is selected.
- **Emoji:** 🙂 opens the emoji picker (also in Chat, next to the message box). Type to search by name ("thumbs", "party", "thanks"), or browse the groups. The ones you use most recently come first, remembered in each browser. From the keyboard: type, then Enter picks the first match, or ↓ and the arrow keys move through them. Escape closes the picker, not the message.
- **Links:** 🔗 or **Ctrl+K** opens the link dialog: the text to show, and a web address or an email address.
  - "example.com" becomes `https://example.com`, and an email address becomes a link that starts a message.
  - Other kinds of address (such as `javascript:`) are refused.
  - **Test this link** opens the address in a new tab.
  - With the cursor in a link, the same button edits it, or **Remove link** takes the link off and keeps the text.
- **Indent:** **Increase / Decrease indent** in the ⋮ menu (or Ctrl+] / Ctrl+[) move the paragraph in 40 px steps, up to 8. In a list they nest or un-nest the item instead. Tab is left free for moving out of the message with the keyboard. The plain-text version indents with spaces.
- **Font:** the menu next to the size menu sets the font for the selected text, or for what you type next: Georgia, Times New Roman, Garamond, Courier New, Verdana, Tahoma, Trebuchet MS, Calibri or Comic Sans MS. **Sans Serif** is the normal font.
  - Each font is sent with fallbacks, for example Calibri, then Arial, so recipients without that font still see something close.
  - The menu shows the font where the cursor is, including fonts quoted from a reply or forward. **Sans Serif** and **Clear formatting** remove it.
- **Font size:** the menu at the start of the toolbar sets **Small, Normal, Large or Huge** (12 / default / 18 / 24 px) for the selected text, or for what you type next. It shows the size where the cursor is. **Normal** and **Clear formatting** remove it. Sizes are sent as part of the text, so common mail apps show them.
- **Text colour:** the **A** button (its bar shows the colour at the cursor) colours the selected text, or what you type next.
  - Choose from 10 colours that read well on white and in mail apps' dark modes. **Automatic** goes back to the normal text colour.
  - Keyboard: Enter opens the palette, the arrow keys move, Enter picks, and Escape closes it without closing the message.
- **Custom colours:** both palettes end with a custom choice. Use the colour well to open your system's colour chooser, or type a code such as `#0b8043`, then **Apply** (or press Enter).
  - A sample shows the result. You're warned, but not stopped, if a text colour would be hard to read on white or a highlight is too dark for the text on it.
  - Your last six custom colours appear in the palette, remembered in that browser.
- **Highlight colour:** the 🖍 button next to it highlights the selected text, like a highlighter pen.
  - Choose from 7 light colours (yellow, green, blue, pink, orange, purple, grey), so the text on them stays readable. **None** removes the highlight.
  - Highlights are sent as a background colour on the text, which Gmail, Outlook and phone mail apps all show. In dark mode the editor keeps dark text on the highlight.
  - Same keyboard use as the text colour button.
- **Tables:** the ▦ button inserts a table. Pick the size from the grid (up to 8 × 8) by hovering and clicking, or with the arrow keys and Enter. **Header row** (on by default) makes the first row bold.
  - Type in a cell, and use Tab / Shift+Tab to move between cells.
  - Inside a table, the toolbar offers **+ Row / − Row / + Column / − Column / Delete table**. Tables can't be put inside other tables.
- **Filters** (Mail → Filters, **Filter messages like this** on any message, or **Create a filter** after a search): rules run by the mail server as mail arrives, so they apply to phones too. They run in order, and can be reordered and switched on or off.
- **Moving filters:** **Export** in the Filters window saves them as *filters.json* (Download your data has the same file, and a Sieve script); **Import…** reads one back on this or another Workspace server, adding only the filters not already there, at the end of the list.
  - Conditions: From, To or Cc, or Subject *contains*, *doesn't contain* or *is exactly* a value; **Message text** *contains* or *doesn't contain* some words; **Mailing list** *contains* (the list's id, such as `news.acme.com`, whoever sends to it); **Size** *is larger than* or *is smaller than* a size (*5 MB*, *300 KB*); **Attachments** *has some* or *has none*. On a newsletter or other mailing-list message, **Filter messages like this** starts from its mailing list rather than the sender. A filter can match all of its conditions or any of them.
  - Actions: move to a folder (created if needed, e.g. `Clients/Acme`), mark as read, star, **show in the Inbox tab** *Primary* or *Updates* (a colleague's build alerts to Updates, a mailing list you care about to Primary), **label it** with one of your [labels](#email) (it shows in Workspace and as a tag in mail apps that show keywords), forward a copy, or delete. Deleting a label takes it out of the filters that used it, and a filter left with nothing else to do is switched off. Filters brought in from another server drop labels that don't exist here.
  - **From a search:** after searching, **Create a filter** (beside *Save this search*) starts a new filter with the search as its conditions: `from:` `to:` `subject:` `has:attachment` `larger:` `smaller:` as they are, `-` as *doesn't contain*, and plain words or "phrases" as words in the message text. What only fits mail already there (`is:unread`, `before:`, `newer_than:`…) is left out, and a message says so. Choose what it does and save (tick **Also apply…** for the mail it found).
  - **Also apply to messages already in the Inbox** (when saving a filter) runs it once over the mail that's there now: matching messages are read, starred, labelled, filed or deleted then and there, and a message says how many (*applied to 12 messages*). Old mail isn't forwarded. Up to 5,000 messages at a time.
  - A matching filter stops later ones unless you untick "Don't apply later filters".
  - Mail a filter files away still gets your vacation reply; mail it deletes doesn't.
  - Forwarded copies keep the original sender. Some receiving servers treat forwarded mail from outside domains with suspicion (SPF), so forwarding works best within your own companies.
- **Forwarding** (Mail → Forwarding): sends a copy of all your incoming mail to another address, such as a colleague's while you're away, or an account you read elsewhere. The address is sent a code from you first, and forwarding starts only once you type it in (*Send a new code* if it's lost; a code lasts 7 days, and a few wrong tries mean sending another), so mail never goes somewhere that didn't agree. Then choose what happens to Workspace's copy: kept in the Inbox, marked read, archived, or moved to the Trash. The mail server does it as mail arrives, so it works with Workspace closed; spam isn't forwarded, and forwarded mail keeps its original sender. Turn it off with the tick box, or **Remove forwarding**; the sidebar says *On* while it's working, and turning it on or off is in the company's activity log.
  - **Companies can keep mail inside** (Admin → Company settings → **Automatic forwarding**): untick *Let people forward their mail automatically to addresses outside the company*, and forwarding (and filters' *forward a copy*) only go to your company's own domains. Forwarding already set up to somewhere else stops at once, and its owner is told why in the Forwarding window.
- **Folders:** create folders and subfolders from the Mail sidebar (**New folder**). Each of your own folders has a ⋮ menu with **Rename** and **Delete**.
  - The built-in folders (Inbox, Drafts, Sent, Archive, Junk, Trash) can't be renamed or deleted.
  - Renaming moves subfolders with it and updates any filter that files mail there.
  - Deleting moves the folder's messages, and its subfolders' messages, to **Trash** first, so nothing is lost by accident. Filters that filed mail there are switched off rather than silently recreating the folder.
  - New and renamed folders are subscribed automatically, including folders a filter creates, so phones and Outlook show them.
- **Signatures** (Mail → Signature): a formatted signature for **each address you can send from**, your own and every alias you belong to (e.g. `sales@`), added below a standard `-- ` separator.
  - Each one can be added to new messages, and to replies and forwards, where it sits above the quoted message.
  - Changing **From** while writing swaps in that address's signature.
  - Replies and forwards default to sending from the address the message was sent to, so a reply to mail for `sales@` goes out from `sales@` with the sales signature.
  - It's inserted when you start writing, so you can still edit it in a particular message, and a reopened draft never gets a second copy.
- **Company signature:** admins write one in **Admin → Company settings → Company signature**, one line per line, with `{name}`, `{job_title}`, `{department}`, `{work_phone}`, `{email}` (the address they send from) and `{company}` filled in for each person (job details come from **Settings → About you** or **Admin → Users → Job and manager**; a line whose details are all missing is left out). **Who gets it:** nobody, people who haven't written their own (they see it in **Signature** and can change it, or go back with **Use the company signature instead**), or everyone (it can't be changed, and people's own signatures are kept for if it's turned off).
- **Pictures in messages:** insert them with the 🖼 button, paste a screenshot, or drag an image file into the message (PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP, up to 10 MB each). Signatures can contain pictures too, such as a logo.
  - **Size:** click a picture, then either drag the handle on its bottom-right corner (any width from 10% to 100% of the message, with a live percentage), or choose **Small, Medium, Large or Full width** (25/50/75/100%) in the toolbar.
    - Dragging snaps to those four sizes when you're within 2%.
    - Keyboard: Tab to the handle and use the arrow keys (5% steps, Shift for 1%), Home for the smallest and End for full width.
    - The size is kept in drafts and shows the same in recipients' mail apps.
    - Pictures without a chosen size show at their natural size, and no picture ever gets wider than a phone screen.
  - **Crop and rotate:** double-click a picture, or click it and choose **✂ Crop / rotate** in the toolbar.
    - **Rotate left / Rotate right** turn it in 90° steps.
    - Drag the crop frame to move it, or its corners and edges to resize it. Choose **Free, Square, 4:3 or 16:9** for the shape; **Reset crop** starts over.
    - Keyboard: Tab to the frame, then use the arrow keys to move it and Shift with the arrow keys to resize it.
    - The new size in pixels shows as you go. **Apply** saves the result to your Drive as "name (edited)" and swaps it into the message at the same size. The original file is left untouched, and Ctrl+Z puts it back.
    - JPEG and WebP photos come out as JPEG, everything else as PNG. An animated GIF becomes a still picture.
    - Only pictures you inserted yourself can be edited, not ones quoted from another email.
  - Pictures are saved to your Drive.
  - When the message is sent they're embedded in the email itself, so recipients see them without access to your server and without "show images" prompts.
  - The plain-text version shows `[image: name]` in their place.
  - Pictures count towards the 25 MB message limit.
  - Replies and forwards carry the original's pictures too (see below).
- **Changes made elsewhere:** what you do on your phone or in Outlook (reading, starring, filing or deleting mail) shows in Workspace within about 10 seconds while Mail is open: the open folder's state is checked every 10 seconds (a quick question to the mail server) and the list reloads only when something changed, straight away when you come back to the tab. New mail shows at once.
- **New-mail notifications:** while Workspace is open in any tab, new mail in your Inbox shows up within a second or two.
  - A pop-up names the sender and subject.
  - The **Mail** icon shows the unread count, and the browser tab title starts with it, e.g. `(3) Workspace`.
  - The open inbox updates by itself.
  - Turn on **Desktop alerts** in the Mail sidebar to get an operating-system notification while Workspace is in a background tab. Clicking it opens the message.
  - Reading mail on a phone or in another tab updates the count too.
  - Behind the scenes, the app keeps one IMAP IDLE connection on the Inbox for each person who has Workspace open, the same push mechanism phones use, and closes it a minute after their last tab closes. Nothing is polled, and watching never marks mail as read.
  - **Other folders:** choose **Notify me about new mail** in a folder's ⋮ menu, which is useful for folders a filter fills, such as `Clients/Acme`.
    - Followed folders show a bell, alerts say which folder ("New mail in Suppliers from …"), and the badge counts unread mail across the Inbox and every followed folder.
    - You can follow up to 10 folders besides the Inbox, which is always followed. Each one uses its own watching connection.
    - Renaming a followed folder keeps it followed; deleting it unfollows it.
    - **The Archive can be followed too** (its ⋮ menu has just this option), which suits filters that file receipts or newsletters straight into it. Sent, Drafts, Junk and Trash can't be followed.
  - Only newly delivered mail triggers an alert. Mail you move yourself (archiving unread mail, "Not spam", a phone tidying folders) keeps its original arrival time, so it counts towards the unread badge but doesn't alert. A message is announced once, even if it lands in the Inbox and is then archived straight away.
- **Show original:** a message's **⋮ → Show original** shows what its headers say: the Message-ID and when it was written, the SPF, DKIM and DMARC results the receiving server recorded (PASS in green, FAIL in red; useful for spotting a forged sender), the servers it passed through with how long each step took, and the full headers (**Copy headers** for IT or a mail provider). **Download message (.eml)** (there, or in the ⋮ menu) saves the whole message as it arrived, for another mail program or as evidence.
- **Share in Chat:** a message's **⋮ → Share in Chat…** puts a card of the email in a Chat conversation you pick (a channel or a direct message), with a message of your own if you like: who it's from and to, the subject and date, the start of what it says (without the quoted history under a reply; **Show more** for the rest, up to about 1,500 characters) and the names of its attachments. It's what the email said when you shared it; the email itself (and its attachments) stays in your mailbox. **Add to Tasks** on a shared email (with no message of its own) makes a task called after its subject, with who sent it and the start of it in the notes. Several at once: tick them in the list (up to 10) and choose **Share in Chat…** in the bar; each goes as its own card, your message with the first. Not from a shared or delegated mailbox. On the card, **Open in Mail** opens the email for anyone there who has it in their own mailbox too (it was sent to them as well, or they sent it); others are told to ask for it to be forwarded. **Reply by email** starts a reply to its sender (*Re: …*) that joins the email's conversation in their mail app. Chat's search finds it by its subject, sender or text; **Download conversation** keeps it (with what it said and its attachments' names, and contacts sent in Chat too); and forwarding the Chat message passes the card on (a contact card too).
- **Forward as attachment:** sends the original email whole, as an `.eml` file with its formatting, pictures, attachments and technical headers (including DKIM) intact. Use it to report a suspicious message to IT, or to pass one on exactly as received.
- **Pictures, tables and colours in replies and forwards:** **Reply**, **Reply all** and a normal **Forward** keep the original's basic formatting (paragraphs, bold, italic, lists, quotes, links), its text colours and highlights, its fonts and font sizes, its alignment and indents, its tables, and the pictures embedded in it, in place. In a reply they sit inside the quote.
  - The server cleans the original first. Scripts, styles, forms and pictures loaded from the internet (including tracking pixels) are left out, so nothing in a forwarded email can run or report back to its sender.
  - While you write, the pictures are shown straight from the original message, so they don't take up space in your Drive. When you send, they're embedded in your email like pictures you insert yourself.
  - You can resize, crop or rotate them like your own pictures. A cropped or rotated picture is saved to your Drive.
  - **Text colours** are kept, such as red for "late" or green for "on track", including colour set on a whole paragraph, list or table cell.
    - Text too light to read on a white page (for example white text from a dark newsletter) is shown in the normal colour instead. Near-black counts as normal text.
    - Only plain colour values are accepted; anything else in the original's styles is dropped.
    - **Highlights** in the original (a light background on words, or `<mark>`) are kept too. Backgrounds on whole paragraphs, table cells or the page (newsletter styling) and dark backgrounds are left out.
    - **Clear formatting** (in the ⋮ menu) removes colours and highlights from the selected text.
  - **Indents** are kept, in the same 40 px steps, for example Outlook's half-inch indents. Gmail's indents (a borderless quote) arrive as indents, not as an extra quote.
  - **Alignment** is kept: centred and right-aligned paragraphs, headings and table cells, and centred pictures, stay where they were. Justified text becomes normal left-aligned text. The Left / Centre / Right buttons show and change it.
  - **Fonts and font sizes** are kept too:
    - Sizes are matched to the size menu's **Small / Normal / Large / Huge**, whatever unit the original used, so a newsletter's tiny print or giant banner stays readable. Headings become Large or Huge.
    - Common mail fonts (Georgia, Times New Roman, Courier New, Verdana, Calibri and others) are kept. The usual sans-serif fonts (Arial, Helvetica, system fonts) count as the normal font. Fonts the recipient may not have fall back to the next common font the original listed.
    - **Clear formatting** removes them as well.
    - In dark mode the editor shows coloured text lighter so it stays readable. The email is sent with the original colours.
  - **Tables:** data tables (an order, an invoice, a schedule) are kept with their header row and merged cells.
    - Newsletters often use tables only to lay out the page. Those are recognised (tables inside tables, single-column tables, or ones marked as layout) and turned into ordinary paragraphs, so a quote doesn't become a grid of boxes.
    - Click inside a table to get **+ Row / − Row / + Column / − Column / Delete table** in the toolbar, to trim what you pass on. Tables you paste into a message work the same way.
    - Sent tables have simple borders that show the same in Gmail, Outlook and phone mail apps. The plain-text version shows each row as `cell | cell`.
  - Plain-text emails are quoted as text, as before.
  - Pictures count towards the 25 MB limit. When replying to a picture-heavy newsletter, delete the quoted pictures you don't need.
  - If the original is deleted before you send, you're told its pictures can't be included.
- **Forwarding with attachments:** forwarding a message brings its attachments along as chips you can remove one by one. They're attached straight from the original message when you send, so nothing is downloaded and re-uploaded. Forward drafts remember them too. If the original has been deleted by the time you send, you're told the attachments can't be included.
- **Vacation reply** (Mail → Vacation reply): an automatic answer with an optional first and last day. It's built on the mail server's standard Sieve "vacation" feature: **Also mark me Out of office in my calendar for these days** (once there's a last day) adds an all-day Out of office event for the same days, in your time zone, so invitations for that time are declined for you as well; it moves when you change the days and goes when you turn the reply off. (The other way round, an Out of office event can turn on the reply.)
  - Each sender gets at most one reply per day.
  - Mailing lists, newsletters, spam and other automatic messages never get one.
  - It also answers mail sent to aliases the user belongs to.
  - Dates use the mail container's `TZ` time zone.
  - The Mail screen shows a banner, with a Turn off button, while it's on.
- **Phones and Outlook:**

  | | Server | Port | Security |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | **Incoming (IMAP)** | your mail hostname | 993 | SSL/TLS |
  | **Outgoing (SMTP)** | your mail hostname | 465 | SSL/TLS (or 587 with STARTTLS) |

  Users sign in with their email address and Workspace password.
- **Accounts that predate email** get a working mailbox password the next time the user signs in to Workspace. Webmail works right away.

**Not built yet:** keeping the original's exact look (block and page backgrounds, page layout, sizes other than the four in the menu) in a normal forward (use **Forward as attachment** to keep everything).

## Download your data

**Settings → Download your data** gives you one .zip of everything you own, in formats other programs open: your **Drive** with its folders (documents as Word, spreadsheets as Excel, presentations as PowerPoint, or as OpenDocument (.odt, .ods, .odp) if that's your choice under **Settings → File format for downloads**; pending suggestions left out), your **calendar** (.ics for Outlook, Google or Apple Calendar), **contacts** (.vcf), **tasks** (*Tasks.csv*: every list with a **List** column, then the columns a list's own download has: section, status, dates, priority, tags, steps, notes; **Import lists from CSV…** in Tasks takes it back), the **history of your lists' tasks** (*Task history.csv*: list, task, when, who, what changed, before and after, oldest first; for reading, not brought back in), **notes** (Markdown: all in *Notes.md*, and each on its own in a `Notes/` folder with its pictures, as Notes' **Download (.zip)** gives them, so **Import notes…** takes them back), **comments** (*Comments.csv*: the ones you wrote anywhere and every one on your own files, with the file, where in it, who, when, whether it's a reply and whether its thread was resolved), **chat** (a `Chat/` folder with a text file for each conversation you're in: direct messages, private channels, and channels you've joined or written in and haven't left, with threads under their message), **meeting chats** (a `Meet chats/` folder with the chat kept from each meeting you started or were in, when its host kept it), **forms** (a `Forms/` folder with each of your forms' questions in Markdown and its responses as a spreadsheet CSV), **mail settings** (a `Mail settings/` folder with your filters as a Sieve script, which most mail servers take, and as JSON, your signatures and email templates as HTML, and your vacation reply), your **event templates** (*Calendar templates.json*) and your **email**: a `Mail/` folder with an .mbox file for each mail folder (*Inbox.mbox*, *Sent.mbox*, *Projects/Acme.mbox*…), the format Thunderbird, Apple Mail and most mail programs import (and that Gmail's own export uses). It says how much there is first (files, events, contacts, tasks, notes, comments, conversations, forms and emails). If the mail server can't be reached at that moment, the rest still downloads and *README.txt* says the email isn't in it. Each download is recorded in the admin activity log. Admins can download the same for anyone in their company (**Admin → Users → ⋮ → Download their data**), for example before deleting the account of someone who has left, or to keep a copy of everything of someone on legal hold. Very large Drives (over 10 GB of uploads) are downloaded folder by folder from Drive instead.

**Bringing it back in:** **Settings → Bring in your data…** takes such a .zip (from this server or another Workspace server, when moving) and adds its **Drive** (as one new folder, *From your data* and the date, with its folders and files as they were: documents as the Word, Excel or PowerPoint files they were saved as; the company's storage limits apply), its **calendar**, **contacts**, **task lists** (each as a new list, with sections, steps and tags) and **notes** (with their pictures) to yours, with your **mail filters**, **email templates**, **signatures** (only where you have none yet: one for an address you don't have here, such as after moving to another domain, becomes your main address's) and **event templates**, in one go; a message says how many of each came in. Events, contacts, filters and templates you already have are skipped, so bringing the same .zip in twice doesn't double them (a filter forwarding outside the company, where that isn't allowed, is left out and said so). Its **email** goes back into your mailbox too, in the background (Mail's *Import mail* shows the progress): each `Mail/` .mbox into the folder it came from (*Clients/Acme.mbox* into Acme inside Clients; Inbox, Sent, Drafts and Archive into yours), messages already there skipped; the .mbox files are kept in a *Mail (.mbox)* folder inside the new Drive folder, so if mail isn't set up on the server (or another import is running) they can be imported later. Bringing it in twice gives a second Drive folder. It's recorded in the activity log. Admins can do the same for someone else: **Admin → Users → ⋮ → Bring in their data…** puts a .zip into that person's account (for example when moving a whole team from another Workspace server, after **Download their data** there); the activity log says whose account it went into. A big .zip is fine: one over about 190 MB is uploaded to your Drive first (with progress shown) and brought in from there, and **Bring in from Drive…** takes one you've already put in Drive. Either way it's read a file at a time, so even a Drive of several GB doesn't have to fit in the server's memory (your storage limit still applies). One brought in from Drive runs in the background on the server: the button shows how far it's got (*Drive (120 of 400)*), it carries on if you close the page, and opening Settings again picks the progress up.

## Two-step verification

Everyone can add a second step to signing in on the web: after the password, a 6-digit code from an authenticator app on their phone (Google Authenticator, Microsoft Authenticator, 1Password, …). Someone who learns a password then still can't get in.

- **Turning it on:** Settings → Two-step verification → **Turn on**. Confirm your password, scan the QR code (or type the key), and enter the code the app shows.
  - You then get **10 recovery codes**. Each signs you in once without your phone. Save them (Copy / Download), because they're shown only once.
  - Other devices that were signed in are signed out.
- **Signing in:** after the password, enter the code (or **Use a recovery code**). Each code works once, and a phone clock up to 30 seconds off is fine.
  - A sign-in allows 5 wrong codes. Wrong codes also count towards sign-in protection below, so guessing gets the sender blocked.
- **Recovery codes running low?** Settings shows how many are left and warns at 3. **New recovery codes** replaces them all.
- **Lost phone:** sign in with a recovery code. Without one, ask an admin: Admin → Users → ⋮ → **Reset two-step verification**. That signs the person out everywhere so they can sign in with their password and set it up again. The user list shows who has **2-step** on.
- **Turning it off** needs your password and a current code (or a recovery code).
- **Requiring it for a company:** Admin → Company settings → **Require two-step verification for everyone in this company**.
  - You need it on your own account first, so the setting can't lock you out.
  - Anyone without it can still sign in with their password, but sees only a setup page until they've set it up. The server refuses everything else for them, including live updates.
  - While it's required, nobody in the company can turn their own off. An admin reset (lost phone) still works; that person then sets it up again at their next sign-in.
  - The settings page shows how many people still have to set it up.
- **Mail apps:** phones and Outlook reading mail over IMAP/SMTP can't ask for a code. Give them a **mail-app password** (below), so your account password alone can't open your mail from a mail app.
- The keys are stored in the database, so they're included in its backups. Protect the backups like the server itself.

## Passkeys

A passkey signs you in with your fingerprint, face, screen lock or a security key instead of your password: nothing to type, nothing to guess, and it only works on this site, so a look-alike phishing page gets nothing.

- **Adding one:** **Settings → Passkeys → Add a passkey**, type your password, name it (*Work laptop*), and your device asks for your fingerprint, face or PIN. Add one on each device you use (up to 20); a password manager that syncs passkeys (iCloud Keychain, Google Password Manager, 1Password…) makes one work on all your devices.
- **Signing in:** on the sign-in page, **🔑 Use a passkey**: the browser offers the passkeys it has for this site, and you're in. No password, and no code from your authenticator app either: the device plus your fingerprint, face or PIN are already two steps. Sign-in protection counts failed passkey sign-ins like wrong passwords, a suspended account is refused, and your company's network rules still apply.
- **Settings → Passkeys** lists them, with when each was added and last used; **Rename** one (*Pixel 9*), or **Remove** it to stop it working (remove it from the device's password manager too).
- **Told when it changes:** adding or removing a passkey puts a notice in your bell (and on your phone, with notifications on) on all your devices, *A passkey was added to your account: “Work laptop”, on Chrome on Windows*, so you'd spot one you didn't add. It's under *Sign-ins from a new device, and passkeys added or removed* in the bell's settings.
- **How it works:** the standard WebAuthn, checked on the server with Node's own cryptography (no extra packages): each sign-in is a fresh challenge signed by the passkey's private key, which never leaves your device; the server keeps only the public half. Passkeys need the site's real address (`PUBLIC_URL`, or the address you open it at) and a secure page (HTTPS, or `localhost`). A company that **requires two-step verification** accepts a passkey as well as an authenticator app; for someone with passkeys and no app, signing in with the password then asks for the passkey as the second step (so the password alone never gets in), and they can't turn the app off unless they have a passkey.
- **Admins:** **Admin → Users** marks people who have one (**🔑 Passkey**), and **⋮ → Reset two-step verification** (a lost phone) removes their passkeys along with their authenticator app.

## Signed-in devices

Settings → **Signed-in devices** lists every browser where you're signed in: what it is ("Chrome on Windows", "Safari on iPhone"), when it was last active, its network address, and when it signed in. The one you're using is marked **This device**.

- **Sign out** ends one device's session; it's signed out at its next click.
- **Sign out all other devices** ends every session except this one.
- If you don't recognise a device, sign it out and change your password. Changing your password, or turning on two-step verification, also signs out all other devices.
- Mail apps aren't listed (they don't keep a web session). Use a mail-app password to control those.
- Sessions from before this version show as "Browser" without details until they next sign in.

## Sign in with Workspace (other apps)

Let people sign in to the company's other apps (Nextcloud, Gitea, Grafana, Outline, a wiki, anything with an **OpenID Connect** or "OAuth2 / OIDC" sign-in option) with their Workspace account.

- **Setting up an app:** Admin → **Apps** → **Add app**: its name and the redirect (callback) address from the app's settings. You get a **client ID** and **client secret** (copy the secret then: it isn't shown again; **New secret** makes another and stops the old one). In the app, give the **issuer** shown on that page (the app finds the rest at `/.well-known/openid-configuration`), the client ID and secret, and the scopes `openid profile email`.
- **Signing in:** the app sends people to Workspace (to sign in first if they aren't); the first time, Workspace asks **Allow** or **Cancel**, saying what the app will see (their name and email address, never their password, mail or files). After that it signs them straight in. Only people in your company can sign in to your company's apps.
- **For the app:** the authorization-code flow (with PKCE when the app uses it), an ID token signed with RS256 (keys at `/oauth/jwks`), and `/oauth/userinfo`. Codes work once, within a minute; access tokens last an hour.
- **More scopes:** `groups` adds the person's groups (Admin → Groups) by name, for apps that give roles by group (say, *Ops admins* are Grafana admins). `offline_access` gives the app a refresh token so it stays signed in without sending people back (30 days; each one works once and brings a new one; it stops when the person removes the app, is suspended, or the app is removed).
- **Who can use an app:** everyone in the company, or (**Edit** → *Only people in these groups*) just some of its groups: say, only *Ops* may sign in to Grafana. Others are told they aren't allowed and to ask an administrator. Someone who leaves the group is signed out of the app the next time it checks (its access and refresh tokens stop working).
- **Company apps on Home:** give an app its own address (**The app's address** when adding or editing it) and it shows under **Company apps** on the Home page of everyone allowed to use it, one click away (they're signed in with Workspace when they get there).
- **Signing out:** an app's *Sign out* can sign people out of Workspace too (the `end_session_endpoint`, `/oauth/logout`, is in the discovery document). When the app sends the ID token it was given, Workspace signs them out at once and sends them back to the app; otherwise Workspace asks first (**Sign out** or **Stay signed in**). People are only ever sent back to addresses on the app's own sites.
- **Activity log:** adding or removing an app, making a new secret, and each time someone allows an app show in Admin → Activity.
- **Taking it back:** everyone sees the apps they've allowed in **Settings → Apps you sign in to** and can remove one (it asks again next time). An admin removing an app stops it for everyone.

## API tokens (scripts)

Your own scripts and tools can use the same API as the web app, as you: a nightly backup that downloads a folder, a scanner that uploads into Drive, a report that reads a spreadsheet.

- **Turning it on (admins):** Admin → Company settings → **Let people make API tokens**. It's off until then, and turning it off stops every token at once (they work again if it's turned back on).
- **Making one:** Settings → **API tokens** → **New token**: what it's for, **Read only** or **Read and write**, and when it expires (30 or 90 days, a year, or never). The token (`nwt_…`) is shown once: copy it then. Only a fingerprint of it is kept, so it can't be shown again.
- **Using it:** send it as `Authorization: Bearer nwt_…`, for example `curl -H "Authorization: Bearer nwt_…" https://your-server/api/files`. Requests with a token don't need the browser's `X-Requested-With` header. A read-only token can only read (GET). A token can't sign in, change the account (anything under `/api/auth`) or make more tokens. It stops working when it's revoked or expires, when its owner is suspended, or when the company turns tokens off; the company's network rules still apply.
- **Keeping track:** the list shows each token's access, when it was made, when it expires, and when and from where it was last used. **Revoke** stops it at once. Admins see every token in the company under Company settings and can revoke any of them. Making and revoking tokens are in the activity log.

## Mail apps (phones, Outlook)

Settings → **Mail apps** shows what to enter in a phone or Outlook: the server name, ports (IMAP 993, SMTP 465 or 587) and user name (your email address).

- **Mail-app password (optional):** **Create mail-app password** gives mail apps their own password, for example `wisn-fyun-ngpb-btim`.
  - It's shown once. Type it exactly, dashes included, or use Copy.
  - From then on mail apps accept only it; your account password stops working in them, within a few seconds. Webmail is unaffected.
  - Someone who learns your account password then can't read your mail from a mail app. A lost phone never had your account password: make a new mail-app password (the old one stops working) or **Remove** it to go back to the account password.
  - Recommended together with two-step verification.
- When an admin resets someone's password (for example after a break-in), their mail-app password is removed too, so only the new password works anywhere.

## Sign-in protection

Once ports 465, 587 and 993 are open, bots will start guessing passwords within hours. The same protection guards the Workspace sign-in page and the mail apps:

- **Blocking:** an internet address that fails **10 sign-ins with different accounts or passwords within 15 minutes** is blocked for **1 hour**. If it does it again within 24 hours, it's blocked for **24 hours**. A blocked address is refused even with the right password, on the web, IMAP and SMTP alike.
- **Same wrong password counts once.** A phone that keeps retrying an old password doesn't get your office blocked.
- **Attacks on one account:** an account attacked from many addresses is **slowed down**, never locked, so attackers can't lock your users out.
- **Never blocked:** this server's own addresses, which includes webmail, and anything you put on the allowlist.
- **Private (LAN) addresses** are slowed down after repeated failures but never blocked.
- **Admin → Security** (platform owner) shows blocked addresses with an **Unblock** button, recent failed sign-ins, and the allowlist. Add your office's fixed IP there.

**How it works:** Dovecot asks the app before and after every IMAP and SMTP sign-in (its "auth policy" feature) on an internal port (8081) that isn't published, using a shared secret. Postfix checks SMTP passwords through Dovecot, so both are covered. If the app is down, mail sign-ins carry on unprotected rather than locking everyone out.

**One thing to check after going live:** the protection needs to see real internet addresses. With normal Docker port publishing, which Container Manager uses, it does. If Admin → Security shows a warning that every failure comes from a private address, your network setup is hiding the real addresses.

## Networks (admins)

**Admin → Company settings → Networks** can limit Workspace to the company's own networks: choose **Only from these addresses and ranges** and list the office's public addresses and VPN ranges (`203.0.113.10`, `198.51.100.0/24`, IPv6 too). From anywhere else, people in the company can't sign in, and anyone already signed in is refused until they're back on one of those networks. Your own current address is shown and filled in, and it must be in the list, so an admin can't shut themselves out; the platform owner is never shut out. Phones and mail apps (IMAP, SMTP, calendar and contact sync) aren't affected. Behind a reverse proxy, set `TRUST_PROXY` so Workspace sees people's real addresses.

## Reports (admins)

**Admin → Reports** shows how the company has used Workspace over the last 7, 30 or 90 days: how many people were active, and for each person when they were last active, files created (new, uploaded or copied), files edited, chat messages sent, meetings organised (events with guests that took place), tasks completed (counted for whoever ticked them off), **video meetings** joined in Meet and **time in video meetings** (only the part within the period), with totals. Above the table, **Video meetings** sums up the company's meetings in Meet over the period: how many were held, the time people spent in them, their average length (from the first person in to the last out) and how many guests without an account joined. Click a column heading to sort by it; **Download CSV** saves the table. Handy for seeing who hasn't started using Workspace yet.

**Active people per day** above the table charts how many people used Workspace each day of the period (signed in, used it in the browser, or synced a phone or mail app), with the average and the busiest day. Point at a day for its count, or **Show as table** for the numbers. Days are in UTC; days from before this report began recording (after updating to this version) are marked and left out of the average.

## Storage (admins)

**Admin → Storage** shows how much of the company's storage is used (and its limit, if the platform owner set one), how much each person uses, split into their files, the earlier versions kept of them, and their trash, biggest users first, and the 25 largest files with their owners (including what's in the trash). Earlier versions and the trash count towards the limit: people can empty their trash in Drive, and delete versions from a file's version history.

With the server's email on, **Mailboxes** below lists each person's mail: how much it takes (all folders, Trash included) and their mailbox size limit if they have one, with how full it is (a red bar from 90%), fullest first. It's asked from the mail server, so it loads on its own and is kept for 10 minutes; **Check again** asks afresh. Limits are set in Admin → Users → ⋮ → **Mailbox size**.

## System health (platform owner)

**Admin → System** (platform owners only) shows whether everything is all right on the NAS: free disk space where the data lives (a warning under 15%, needs attention under 5% or 1 GB), the daily database snapshots (a warning when the newest is over 30 hours old, needs attention after two days), whether the mail server answers, and its TLS certificate: days left (a warning under three weeks, needs attention under a week or once expired) and whether it's self-signed. It also lists the version, how long the server has been running, and how big the database, files and snapshots are, with the number of companies and people. The same checks run every hour: platform owners get a notification (**Server health** in notification settings) for each problem, at most once a day, and again if a fixed problem comes back. Below the checks:

- **Mail DNS records** checks every domain's records (MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, and the others Admin → Email → **Check DNS** lists) and that the mail host's reverse DNS matches, looked up again every few hours. Missing MX, SPF or DKIM, or reverse DNS that doesn't match, needs attention, since mail then isn't delivered or is widely refused; the others are a warning.
